\rightarrow~

btm
.

empty

:

env
.

dot

:

env

\rightarrow~

ctm

\rightarrow~

env
.

|
def
}]

Figure 2:
Closure Converted Language

Concretely,
tm
is the type of our input language.
Applications are again represented by the constructor
app
that
takes two terms, the first represents the function and the second its
parameter.

One option would be to simply use the same representation for the
target language that we used for the source language (i.e.: the
untyped
\lambda
-calculus). A better option is to use a new
representation that highlights that functions in the target language
do no depend on their environments. To this effect we declare in
Figure
2
two types,
ctm
to represent terms
after the conversion and
btm
to represent the bodies of
functions. We take advantage of the expressive power of the
specification framework SF to define the closed bodies of the
functions
btm
and the converted terms
ctm
.

Applications in the target language are defined using the constructor

capp
and simply take two target terms to form an
application. But functions (constructor
clam
) take a

btm
object wrapped in
{}
braces. This means
that the object inside the braces is closed. The curly braces denote
the internal closed modality of the syntactic framework. As the
original functions may depend on variables in the environment, we need
closures where we pair a function with an environment that provides
the appropriate instances for variables. We define our own environment
explicitly, because they are part of the target language and the
built-in substitution is an operation on terms that is eagerly
computed away. Inside the body of the function, we need to bind all
the variables from the environment that the body uses such that later
we can instantiate them applying the substitution. This is achieved by
defining multiple bindings using constructors
bind
and

embed
inside the term.

When writing a function that translates between representations, the
open terms depend on contexts that store assumptions of different
representations. Therefore, it is often the case that one needs to
relate these contexts. In our example here we define a context
relation that keeps the input and output contexts in sync using a GADT
data type
rel
in OCaml where we model contexts as types.
The relation statically checks correspondence between contexts, but it
is also available at run-time (i.e. after type-erasure). It states
that for each variable in the source contexts there is a corresponding
one in the target context.

⬇

type

(
_

,

_
)

rel

=

Empty

:

([.],

[.])

rel

|

Both

:

([
\gamma
],

[
\delta
])

rel

\rightarrow~

([
\gamma
,

x
:
tm
],

[
\delta
,

y
:
ctm
])

rel

exception

Error

of

string

let

rec

lookup

[
@type

\gamma

\delta
.[
\gamma

\vdash\,

tm
]
\rightarrow~
(
\gamma
,

\delta
)

rel
\rightarrow~
[
\delta

\vdash\,

ctm
]]

=

fun

t

\rightarrow~

function

|

Both

r
’

\rightarrow~

begin

match

|

{
p
|

,

\vdash\,

x

|
p
}

\rightarrow~

{
t
|
_
,

\vdash\,

x
|
t
}

|

{
p
|

_
,
x

\vdash\,

#

|
p
}

\rightarrow~

let

v1

=

lookup

{
t
|
#v
|
t
}

r
’

in

{
t
|
_
,

\vdash\,

’

[
_
]|

}

|

\rightarrow~

(‘‘
Term

that

is

not

a

|

\rightarrow~

(‘‘

is

’’)

end

The function
lookup
searches for related variables in the
context relation. If we have a source context

\gamma
,
x
:
tm
and a target context

\delta
,
y
:
ctm
, then we consider two variable cases: In
the first case, we use matching to check that we are indeed looking
for the top-most variable
x
and we simply return the
corresponding target variable. If we encounter a variable from the
context, written as
#
#v
, then we recurse in the smaller
context stripping off the variable declaration
x
. Note
that
#
#v
denotes a variable from the context

_
, that is not
x
, while
#v

describes a variable from the context
_
,

x
, i.e. it could
be also
x
. The recursive call returns the corresponding
variable
v1
in the target context that does not include
the variable declaration
x
. We hence need to weaken

v1
to ensure it is meaningful in the original context. We
therefore associate
’
v1
with the identity substitution for
the appropriate context, namely:
[
_
]
. In this case, it
will be elaborated into a one variable shift in the internal syntax
(i.e.:
\uparrow^{1}
). The last case returns an exception whenever we
are trying to look up in the context something that is not a variable.

As we cannot express at the moment in the type annotation that the
input to the lookup function is indeed only a variable from the
context
\gamma
and not an arbitrary term, we added another fall-through
case for when the context is empty. In this case the input term cannot
be a variable, as it would be out of scope.

Finally, we implement the function
conv
which takes an
untyped source term in a context
\gamma
and a relation of
source and target variables, described by
(
\gamma
,

\delta
)

rel
and
returns the corresponding target term in the target context

\delta
.

⬇

let

[
@type

.

,

)

[

\rightarrow~

|

{

|

(\

.

)

|

\rightarrow~

(

)

{

|

.

{

{’

{

\rightarrow~

{

|

}

{

\rightarrow~

{

}

The core of the translation is defined in functions
conv
,

envr
, and
close
. The main function is

conv
. It is implemented by recursion on the source
term. There are three cases: i) source variables simply get translated
by looking them up in the context relation, ii) applications just get
recursively translated each term in the application, and iii)
lambda expressions are translated recursively by
converting the body of the expression in the extended context (notice the
recursive call with
Both

r
) and then turning
the lambda expression into a closure.

In the first step we generate the closed body by the function

close
that adds the multiple binders (constructors

bind
and
embed
) and generates the
closed term. Note that the return type
[
btm
]
of
close
guarantees that
the final result is indeed a closed term, because we omit the context.
For clarity, we could have written
[

\vdash\,

btm
]
.

Finally, the function
envr

computes the substitution (represented by the type

env
) for the closure.

The implementation of closure conversion shows how to enforce closed
terms in the specification, and how to make contexts and their
relationships explicit at run-time using OCaml’s GADTs. We believe it
also illustrates well how HOAS trees can be smoothly manipulated and
integrated into OCaml programs that may use effects.

3
Core-ML
: A Functional Language with Pattern Matching and Data Types

We now introduce
Core-ML
, a functional language based on ML with pattern
matching and data types. In Section
5
we will extend this
language to also support contextual types and terms in our syntactic
framework SF.

We keep the language design of
Core-ML
minimal in the interest of
clarity. However, our prototype implementation which we describe in
Section
9
supports interaction with all of OCaml’s
features such as exceptions, references and GADTs.

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Types}&\tau&\mathrel{::=}&D\mathrel{\mid}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Expressions}&e&\mathrel{::=}&i\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e\mathrel{\mid}\\[1.99997pt]
&&&{\texttt{let}}\,x=i\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Neutral Exp.}&i&\mathrel{::=}&{i}\,{e}\mathrel{\mid}{C}\,{\overrightarrow{e}}\mathrel{\mid}x\mathrel{\mid}e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Patterns}&pat&\mathrel{::=}&{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}\mathrel{\mid}x\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Branches}&b&\mathrel{::=}&\mid pat\mapsto e\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Contexts}&\Gamma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\\
\mbox{Signature}&\Xi&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Xi,D{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{type}}\mathrel{\mid}\Xi,C{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\overrightarrow{\tau}\to D\\
\end{array}

In
Core-ML
, we declare data-types by adding type formers (
D
) and type
constructors (
C
) to the signature (
\Xi
). Constructors must
be fully-applied. In addition all functions are named and recursive.
The language supports pattern matching with nested patterns where
patterns consist of just variables and fully applied constructors. We
assume that all patterns are linear (i.e. each variable occurs at most
once) and that they are covering.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau}:\mbox{Expression $e$ checks against type $\tau$ in context $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\to\tau^{\prime}\Gamma,f:\tau\to\tau^{\prime},x:\tau\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau^{\prime}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{let}}\,x=i\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau^{\prime}\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\forall b_{k}\in\overrightarrow{b}\mathrel{.}\Gamma\vdash b_{k}\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau^{\prime}\to\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\tau=\tau^{\prime}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau}:\mbox{Neutral expr. $i$ synthesizes type $\tau$ in context $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\par\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash{i}\,{e}\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau^{\prime}\to\tau\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau^{\prime}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash x\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau\Gamma(x)=\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{C}\,{\overrightarrow{e}}\mathrel{\Rightarrow}D\lx@proof@logical@and\Xi(C)=\overrightarrow{\tau}\to D\forall\tau_{i}\in\overrightarrow{\tau}\mathrel{.}\forall e_{i}\in\overrightarrow{e}\mathrel{.}\Gamma\vdash e_{i}\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau_{i}\par\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\mid pat\mapsto e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}}:\mbox{Branch checks against $\tau_{1}$ and $\tau_{2}$ in $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma\vdash\mid pat\mapsto e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau^{\prime}\to\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\downarrow\Gamma}:\mbox{Pattern $pat$ is of type $\tau$ and binds variables in context $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}{x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\vdash{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}D\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma_{1},...,\Gamma_{i}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Xi(C)=\overrightarrow{\tau}\to D~~\forall\tau_{i}\in\overrightarrow{\tau}\mathrel{.}\forall pat_{i}\in\overrightarrow{pat}\mathrel{.}~~\vdash pat_{i}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{i}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma_{i}}\end{array}

Figure 3:
Core-ML
Typing Rules

The bi-directional typing rules for
Core-ML
have access to a signature

\Xi
and are standard (see Fig.
3
) and the signature
remains unchanged throughout the rules.

There are three things to notice in the rules:

1.

The rule
t-constr
ensures that the constructor
C

is fully applied.

2.

When type checking pattern matching in rule
t-match
the
branches are checked against the type that the pattern needs to have
(i.e.:
\tau^{\prime}
) and the type the body needs to have (i.e.:
\tau
).
The use of the arrow in this context is an overloading of syntax to
mean the type of the pattern and the type of the body.

3.

Finally, in the rule for branches(
t-branch
) we check the
body in the context
\Gamma
extended with the context from the
pattern of the branch (context
\Gamma^{\prime}
).

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Values}&v&\mathrel{::=}&{C}\,{\overrightarrow{v}}\mathrel{\mid}({\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e)[\rho]\\
\mbox{Environments}&\rho&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\rho,v/x\\
\mbox{Closures}&L&\mathrel{::=}&e[\rho]\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{e[\rho]\Downarrow v}:\mbox{Expression $e$ evaluates to value $v$ in environment $\rho$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
({i}\,{e})[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and i[\rho]\Downarrow({\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e^{\prime})[\rho^{\prime}]e[\rho]\Downarrow v^{\prime}e^{\prime}[\rho^{\prime},v^{\prime}/x]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
x[\rho]\Downarrow v\rho(x)=v\par\quad\par({\texttt{let}}\,x=i\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e)[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and i[\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}e[\rho,v_{1}/x]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ {\mid pat_{1}\mapsto e_{b}}\mathrel{::}\overrightarrow{b})[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and i[\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}\vdash{pat_{1}}\,\cancel{\doteq}\,v({\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b})[\rho]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ {\mid pat_{1}\mapsto e_{b}}\mathrel{::}\overrightarrow{b})[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and i[\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}\hat{\Gamma}\vdash{pat_{1}}\doteq v/\rho^{\prime}e_{b}[\rho,\rho^{\prime}]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({C}\,{\overrightarrow{e}}\,)[\rho]\Downarrow{C}\,{\overrightarrow{v}}\overrightarrow{e}\,[\rho]\Downarrow\overrightarrow{v}\par\quad\par e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau[\rho]\Downarrow ve[\rho]\Downarrow v\par\quad\par i[\rho]\Downarrow vi[\rho]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e)[\rho]\Downarrow({\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e)[\rho]\end{array}

Figure 4:
Core-ML
Big-Step Operational Semantics

In Figure
4
we define the operational semantics using
an environment based approach. We define:

•

values, that are either constructors applied to other values or
recursive functions.

•

environments that assign a value to each variable in the context.

•

closures that represent pending computations in the
environment. We write
e[\rho]
for a closure that consists of
expression
e
in context
\rho
.

We use an environment based operational semantics to avoid having to
define substitutions at the level of
Core-ML
and later, substitution of
quoted variables. This agrees with Babybel that in its implementation
we do not need to define quoted variable substitution as we reuse
OCaml’s substitution so this style of semantics seems fitting.

The evaluation judgment
e[\rho]\Downarrow v
means that the expression

e
in environment
\rho
evaluates in a big step to value
v
. The
more interesting rules are the ones for pattern matching that use
first-order matching as defined in Fig.
5
. As
traditional, in a
match
expression, patterns are matched branch
by branch until one matches and then the body is executed in the
extended environment that resulted from the matching.

We characterize both matching and failure to match. Successful
matching is defined by the judgment
\Gamma\vdash pat\doteq v/\rho

that means that the pattern
pat
with unification variables in

\Gamma
and the value
v
match producing the substitution
\rho
.
Failure to match is defined by the judgment
\vdash pat\,\cancel{\doteq}\,v
that
states that the pattern
pat
with unification variables in
\Gamma

and the value
v
cannot be matched. Since
v
is always ground we do
not need unification but matching. We also will not address the
details of pattern matching compilation but merely state that it is
possible to implement it in an efficient manner using decision
trees
(
Augustsson 1985
)
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\hat{\Gamma}\vdash pat\doteq v/\rho}:\mbox{Value $v$ matches $pat$ producing substitution $\rho$ }\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
x\vdash x\doteq v/(\cdot,v/x)\par\\[10.00002pt]
\hat{\Gamma}\vdash{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}\doteq{C}\,{\overrightarrow{v}}/\rho_{0},\dots\rho_{n-1}\lx@proof@logical@and n=|\overrightarrow{pat}|\hat{\Gamma}=\hat{\Gamma}_{0},\dots,\hat{\Gamma}_{n-1}\forall i<n.\hat{\Gamma}_{i}\vdash\ pat_{i}\doteq v_{i}/\rho_{i}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\vdash pat\,\cancel{\doteq}\,v}:\mbox{Value $v$ does not match pattern $pat$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\vdash{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}\,\cancel{\doteq}\,{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}k\neq m\par\quad\par\vdash pat\,\cancel{\doteq}\,{\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e\par\\[10.00002pt]
\vdash{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}\,\cancel{\doteq}\,{C}\,{\overrightarrow{v}}\lx@proof@logical@and n=|\overrightarrow{pat}|\exists i<n.\ pat_{i}\,\cancel{\doteq}\,v_{i}\end{array}

Figure 5:
First-order Matching

4
A Syntactic Framework

In this section we describe the Syntactic Framework (SF) based on the
modal logic S4
(
Davies and Pfenning 2001
)
. Our framework characterizes only
normal forms. All computation is delegated to the ML layer, that will
perform pattern matching and substitutions on terms.

1
The definition of SF

The Syntactic Framework (SF) is a simply typed
\lambda
-calculus
based on S4 where the type system forces all variables to be of base
type, and all constants declared in a signature
\Sigma
to be fully
applied. This simplifies substitution as variables of base type cannot
be applied to other terms, and in consequence, there is no need for
hereditary substitution in the specification language. Finally, the
syntactic framework supports the box type to describe closed terms

(
Pfenning and Davies 2001
)
. It can also be viewed as a restricted version of
the contextual modality in
(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)
which could be an
interesting extension to our work.

Having closed objects enforced at the specification level is not
strictly necessary. However, being able to state that some objects are
closed in the specification has two distinct advantages: first, the
user can specify some objects as closed so their contexts are always
empty. This removes the need for some unnecessary substitutions.
Second, it allows us to encode more fine-grained invariants and is
hence an important specification tool (i.e. when implementing closure
conversion in Section
1
).

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Types}&A,B&\mathrel{::=}&\textbf{a}\mathrel{\mid}A\to B\mathrel{\mid}\square\,A\\
\mbox{Terms}&M,N&\mathrel{::=}&{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}\mathrel{\mid}\lambda\,{x}.M\mathrel{\mid}\{M\}\mathrel{\mid}x\\
\mbox{Contexts}&\Psi,\Phi&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\\
\mbox{Signature}&\Sigma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Sigma,\textbf{a}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K\mathrel{\mid}\Sigma,\,\textbf{c}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\end{array}

Fig.
6
shows the typing rules for the syntactic
framework. Note that constructors always are fully applied (as per
rule
t-con
), and that all variables are of base type as enforced
by rules
t-var
and
t-lam
.

The specification framework’s terms are manipulated by the computational language, in
the resulting system any concrete use of terms in the syntactic
framework will be done by pattern matching and the application of
substitutions in the computational language therefore we will not have
any elimination forms in the syntactic framework.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A}:\mbox{$M$ has type $A$ in context $\Psi$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Psi\vdash\lambda\,{x}.M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\to A\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\par\quad\par\Psi\vdash\{M\}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\square\,A\cdot\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\par\quad\par\Psi\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\Psi(x)=\textbf{a}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\lx@proof@logical@and\Sigma(\textbf{c})=A\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A/\textbf{a}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A/B}:\mbox{spine $\overrightarrow{M}$ checks against type $A$ and has target type $B$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\quad\par\Psi\vdash\cdot{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}/\textbf{a}\par\quad\par\Psi\vdash{N}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\to B/\textbf{a}\lx@proof@logical@and\Psi\vdash N{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}B/\textbf{a}\par\end{array}

Figure 6:
Syntactic Framework Typing

2
Contextual Types

We use contextual types
(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)
to embed possibly open
SF objects in
Core-ML
and ensure that they are well-scoped. We use
contextual types where we pair the type
A
of an SF object together
with its surrounding context
\Psi
in which it makes sense. This
follows the design of Beluga
(
Pientka 2008
;
Cave and Pientka 2012
)
.

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Contextual Types}&U&\mathrel{::=}&[\Psi\vdash A]\\
\mbox{Type Erased Contexts}&\hat{\Psi}&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\hat{\Psi},x\\
\mbox{Contextual Objects}&C&\mathrel{::=}&[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\\
\end{array}

Contextual objects, written as
[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]
pair the term

M
with the variable name context
\hat{\Psi}
to allow for

\alpha
-renaming of variables occurring in
M
. Note how the

\hat{\Psi}
context just corresponds to the context with the typing
assumptions erased.

When we embed contextual objects in a programming language we want to
refer to variables and expressions from the ambient language, in order
to support incomplete terms. Following

(
Nanevski et al. 2008
;
Pientka 2008
)
, we extend our syntactic
framework SF with two ideas: first, we have incomplete terms with
meta-variables to describe holes in terms. As in Beluga, there are two
different kinds:
quoted variables

’u
represent a
hole in the term that may be filled by an arbitrary term. In contrast,

parameter variables
represent a hole in a term that
may be filled only with some bound variable from the context.
Concretely, a parameter variable may be
#x
and describe any
concrete variable from a context
\Psi
. We may also want to restrict
what bound variables a parameter variable describes. For example, if
we have two sharp signs (i.e.
##x
) the top-most variable
declaration is excluded. Intuitively, the number of sharp signs, after
the first, in front of
x
correspond to a weakening (or in
de Bruijn lingo the number of shifts). Second, substitution operations
allow us to move terms from one context to another.

We hence extend the syntactic framework SF with quoted variables,
parameter variables and closures, written as
M[\sigma]
.
We annotate the substitution with its domain and range to simplify the
typing rule, however our prototype omits these typing annotations and
lets type inference infer them.

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Parameter Variables}&{\texttt{v}}&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{\#x}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{\#\#x}}\\
\mbox{Terms}&M&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{'u}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{v}}\mathrel{\mid}M[\sigma]\\
\mbox{Substitutions}&\sigma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\sigma,M/x\\
\mbox{Ambient Ctx.}&\Gamma&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}\Gamma,{\texttt{u}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\\
\end{array}

In addition, we extend the context
\Gamma
of the ambient language

Core-ML
to keep track of assumptions that have a contextual type.

Finally, we extend the typing rules of the syntactic framework SF to
include quoted variables, parameter variables, closures, and
substitutions. We keep all the previous typing rules for SF from
Section
4
where we thread through the ambient
\Gamma
,
but the rules remain unchanged otherwise.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{v}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}}:\mbox{Parameter Variable ${\texttt{v}}$ has type $\textbf{a}$ in contexts
$\Psi$ and $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#x}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\Gamma(x)=[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi,y{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\_\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#v}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{v}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A}:\mbox{Term
$M$ has type $A$ in contexts $\Psi$ and $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash{\texttt{'u}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\Gamma({\texttt{u}})=[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash{\texttt{v}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{v}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M[\sigma]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi\Gamma;\Phi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Psi^{\prime}}:\mbox{Substitution $\sigma$ from $\Psi^{\prime}$ to$\Psi$ in the amb. ctx. $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\cdot{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\cdot\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma,M/x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\Psi^{\prime},x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a})\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Psi^{\prime}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\end{array}

The rules for quoted and parameter variables (
t-qvar
and

t-pvar
respectively) might seem very restrictive as we can only
use a meta-variable of type
\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}
in the same context

\Psi
. As a consequence meta-variables often occur as a closure
paired with a substitution (i.e.:
{{\texttt{'u}}}\,{[\sigma]}
). This leads to
the following admissible rule:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash{{\texttt{'u}}}\,{[\sigma]}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma({\texttt{u}})=[\Phi\vdash\textbf{a}]\Delta;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi\end{array}

We stress
that
M[\sigma]
it is an operation that is applied and not part of the syntax of
our terms.

Note that when we compile SF objects, this substitution will be
eagerly applied in order to keep terms in the syntactic framework in
normal form.

Substitution is straightforward to define. We write it here as a
prefix, to stress that it is an operation that is applied and not part
of the term. Substitutions (
[\_]\_
) is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}[\sigma](\lambda\,{x}.M)&=&\lambda\,{x}.{(M[\sigma,x/x])}\\
[\sigma]\,\{M\}&=&\{M\}\\
[\sigma]\,{\texttt{'u}}&=&{\texttt{'u}}\\
[\sigma]\,{\texttt{\#x}}&=&{\texttt{\#x}}\\
[\sigma]\,x&=&{\texttt{lookup}}\ x\ \sigma\\
[\sigma](M[\phi])&=&[\sigma\circ\phi]\,M\\
[\sigma]({\textbf{c}}\,{\vec{M}})&=&{\textbf{c}}\,{\vec{N}}\quad\text{where}\ {N_{i}=([\sigma]{M_{i}})}\\
[\sigma]\{M\}&=&\{M\}\end{array}

We distinguish the actual operation of applying a substitution from
the explicit substitution in a term, by writing the substitution in a
prefix position.

Composing substitutions is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rrccl}\sigma&\circ&\phi,M/x&=&(\sigma\circ\phi),([\sigma]\,M)/x\\
\sigma&\circ&\cdot&=&\sigma\\
\end{array}

The lookup of variables in substitutions is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rrrcl}{\texttt{lookup}}&x&\sigma,M/x&=&M\\
{\texttt{lookup}}&x&\sigma,M/y&=&{\texttt{lookup}}\ x\ \sigma\\
\end{array}

Note that looking up in an empty substitution is not defined as it is
ill-typed.

The next step is to define the embedding of this framework in a
programming language that will provide the computational power to
analyze and manipulate contextual objects.

5
Core-ML
with Contextual Types

To embed contextual SF objects into
Core-ML
, we extend the syntax of

Core-ML
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{Types}&\tau&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\\
\mbox{Expressions}&e&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{cmatch}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{c}\\
\mbox{Patterns}&pat&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}[\hat{\Psi}\vdash R]\\
\mbox{Contextual Branches}&c&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\mathrel{\mid}\mid[\Psi\vdash R]\mapsto e\\
\end{array}

In particular, we allow programmers to use the expression:

{\texttt{cmatch}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{c}

to directly pattern match on the syntactic structures they define in
SF.

1
SF Objects as SF Patterns

We allow programmers to analyze SF objects directly via pattern
matching. The grammar of SF patterns follows the grammar of SF
objects.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{SF Parameter Pattern}&{\texttt{w}}&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{\#p}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{\#\#w}}\\
\mbox{SF Patterns}&R&\mathrel{::=}&\lambda\,{x}.R\mathrel{\mid}\{R\}\mathrel{\mid}x\mathrel{\mid}{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{R}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{'u}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{w}}\\
\end{array}

However, there is an important restriction: closures are not allowed
in SF patterns. Intuitively this means that all quoted variables are
associated with the identity substitution and hence depend on the
entire context in which they occur. Parameter variables may be
associated with weakening substitutions. This allows us to easily
infer the type of quoted variables and parameter variables as we type
check a pattern. This is described by the judgment:

\boxed{\Psi\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma}:\mbox{Pattern $R$ has type $A$ in $\Psi$ and binds $\Gamma$}

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{w}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}}:\mbox{Parameter Pattern ${\texttt{w}}$ has type $\textbf{a}$ in $\Psi$ and binds $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#p}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{p:[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]}\par\quad\par\Psi,y{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\_\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#w}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{w}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Psi\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma}:\mbox{Pattern $R$ has type $A$ in $\Psi$ and binds $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash\lambda\,{x}.R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\to A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\par\quad\par\Psi\vdash\{R\}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\square\,A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\cdot\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash{\texttt{'u}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{u{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{R}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}\lx@proof@logical@and\Sigma(\textbf{c})=A\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{R}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A/\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash{\texttt{w}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}\Psi\vdash_{v}{\texttt{w}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}\quad\par\Psi\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\cdot}\Psi(x)=\textbf{a}\par\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A/B\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}}:\mbox{Pat. Spine $\overrightarrow{R}$ has type $A$ and target $B$ and binds $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Psi\vdash\cdot{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}/\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}\cdot\par\quad\par\Psi\vdash{N}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\to B/\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Psi\vdash N{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}B/\textbf{a}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}

Figure 7:
Typing Rules for SF Patterns

Figure
7
shows the typing rules for SF patterns. They
closely follow the typing of SF terms. The more interesting ones are
the parameter patterns as they illustrate the built-in weakening.

Further, the matching algorithm for SF patterns degenerates to simple
first-order matching
(
Pientka and Pfenning 2003
)
and can be defined
straightforwardly. However, it is worth considering the matching rules for parameter
patterns. As matching will only consider well-typed terms, we know
that in the rules
m-pv
and
m-pv-#
the variable
x
is
well-typed in the context
\hat{\Psi}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash_{v}w\doteq x/\rho}:\mbox{Param. Patt. $w$ matches var. $x$ from $\hat{\Psi}$
producing $\rho$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\texttt{p}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[\Psi\vdash A];\hat{\Psi}\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#p}}\doteq x/\cdot,[\hat{\Psi}\vdash x]/{\texttt{p}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;{\hat{\Psi},y}\vdash_{v}{\texttt{\#w}}\doteq x/\rho\lx@proof@logical@and x\neq y\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash_{v}w\doteq x/\rho\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash R\doteq M/\rho}:\mbox{$M$ matches pattern $R$ with vars. in $\hat{\Psi}$
producing $\rho$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash\lambda\,{x}.R\doteq\lambda\,{x}.M/\rho\Gamma;\hat{\Psi},x\vdash R\doteq M/\rho\par\quad\par\cdot;\hat{\Psi}\vdash x\doteq x/\cdot\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash\{R\}\doteq\{M\}/\rho\Gamma;\cdot\vdash R\doteq M/\rho\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{R}}\doteq{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}/\rho_{0},\dots,\rho_{n}\text{for all }R_{i}\in\overrightarrow{R}\text{ such as }\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash R_{i}\doteq M_{i}/\rho_{i}\par\\[10.00002pt]
{\texttt{u}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[\Psi\vdash A];\hat{\Psi}\vdash{\texttt{'u}}\doteq M/\cdot,[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]/{\texttt{u}}\par\quad\par\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash{\texttt{w}}\doteq x/\rho\Gamma;\hat{\Psi}\vdash_{v}{\texttt{w}}\doteq x/\rho\par\end{array}

Finally, it has another important consequence: closures only appear in
the branches of case-expressions. As
Core-ML
has a call-by-value
semantics, we know the instantiations of quoted variables and
parameter variables when they appear in the body of a case-expression
and all closures are eliminated by applying the substitution eagerly.
Given these conditions the matching operation remains first-order. An
alternative way of explaining this is that in fact we have a
degenerate case of the pattern fragment
(
Miller 1991
)
of
higher-order unification where all unification variables are applied
to different variables and all the variables in the context. In this
situation we only need first order
matching
(
Pientka and Pfenning 2003
)
, which enables efficient
pattern matching compilation.

2
Typing Rules for
Core-ML
with Contextual Types

We now add the following typing rules for contextual objects and
pattern matching to the typing rules of
Core-ML
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\mathrel{\Leftarrow}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\par\\[6.99997pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{cmatch}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\forall b\in\overrightarrow{b}\mathrel{.}\Gamma\vdash b\mathrel{\Leftarrow}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\to\tau\par\\[6.99997pt]
\Gamma\vdash[\Psi\vdash R]\mapsto e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]\to\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Psi\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\downarrow\Gamma^{\prime}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau\end{array}

The typing rule for contextual objects (rule
t-ctx-obj
) simply
invokes the typing judgment for contextual objects. Notice, that we
need the context
\Gamma
when checking contextual objects, as they
may contain quoted variables from
\Gamma
.

Extending the operational semantics to handle contextual SF objects is
also straightforward.

Additionally, we need rules to evaluate the matching of contextual
types that largely follow the rules for pattern matching

e-match-succ
and
e-match-fail
but use the matching operation
defined for contextual objects.

Finally, the operational semantics needs to be extended with rules to
support the new constructs. The rules, in Figure
8
for
contextual terms simply take the terms apart and re-build them,
except for quoted variables that are looked up in the environment(rule

ec-qvar
) and for terms with substitutions where the substitution
operation is immediately applied(rule
ec-sub
). The rules for the new pattern matching construct are analogous to the
ones from
Core-ML
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{e[\rho]\Downarrow v}:\mbox{Expression $e$ evaluates to value $v$ in environment $\rho$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
[\hat{\Psi}\vdash\lambda\,{x}.M][\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash\lambda\,{x}.N][\hat{\Psi},x\vdash M][\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi},x\vdash N]\par\\[10.00002pt]
[\hat{\Psi}\vdash\{M\}][\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash\{N\}][\cdot\vdash\{M\}][\rho]\Downarrow[\cdot\vdash\{N\}]\par\\[10.00002pt]
[\hat{\Psi}\vdash x][\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash x]\par\\[10.00002pt]
[\hat{\Psi}\vdash{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}][\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{N}}]\forall M\in\overrightarrow{M}.\ M[\rho]\Downarrow N\par\\[10.00002pt]
{\texttt{'u}}[\rho]\Downarrow M\rho({\texttt{u}})=M\par\quad\par{\texttt{\#x}}[\rho]\Downarrow y\rho({\texttt{x}})=y\par\quad\par{\texttt{\#\#x}}[\rho,M/z]\Downarrow y\rho({\texttt{x}})=y\par\par\\[10.00002pt]
M[\sigma][\rho]\Downarrow N([\sigma]M)[\rho]\Downarrow N\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{cmatch}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ {\mid[\Psi\vdash R]\mapsto e_{b}}\,\overrightarrow{c})[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e[\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\Psi\vdash R\,\cancel{\doteq}\,M({\texttt{cmatch}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{c})[\rho]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{cmatch}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ {\mid[\Psi\vdash R]\mapsto e_{b}}\,\overrightarrow{c})[\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e[\rho]\Downarrow[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\Gamma^{\prime};\Psi\vdash R\doteq M/\rho^{\prime}e_{b}[\rho,\rho^{\prime}]\Downarrow v\par\end{array}

Figure 8:
Extended Operational Semantics

6
Core-ML
with GADTs

So far, in this chapter, we reviewed how to support contextual types
and contextual objects in a standard functional programming language.
This allows us to define syntactic structures with binders and
manipulate them with the guarantee that variables will not escape
their scopes. This brings some of the benefits of the Beluga system to
mainstream languages focusing on writing programs instead of proofs. A
naive implementation of this language extension requires augmenting
the type checker and operational semantics of the host language. This
is a rather significant task – especially if it includes implementing
a compiler for the extended language. However, we can take advantage
of the powerful type-system in modern functional languages to make the
implementation more straight forward. Concretely, we use Generalized
Abstract Data Types (GADTs) as a target to our translation. GADTs are
a generalization of algebraic data-types that allow types to be
indexed by other types. This mild form type dependency is enough to
implement the SF. In the literature, GADTs have been introduced
several times under different names: phantom
types
(
Cheney and Hinze 2003b
)
, guarded recursive
types
(
Xi et al. 2003
)
and equality types
(
Sheard and Pasalic 2008
)
.
In this section, we describe how
to embed
Core-ML
with contextual types in a functional language with
GADTs, called
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
, based on
\lambda_{2,G\mu}

by
Xi et al. 2003
. The choice of this target language is
motivated by the fact that it is close to what realistic typed
languages already offer (e.g.: OCaml and Haskell) and it directly
lends itself to an implementation.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{Signatures}&\Sigma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Sigma,D{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,\dots,*)\to*\mathrel{\mid}{C}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}\mathrel{.}\tau\to D[\overrightarrow{\tau}]\\
\mbox{Types}&\tau&\mathrel{::=}&D[\overrightarrow{\tau}]\mathrel{\mid}\forall{\alpha}\mathrel{.}\tau\mathrel{\mid}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\mathrel{\mid}\alpha\mathrel{\mid}\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\\
\mbox{Expressions}&e&\mathrel{::=}&x\mathrel{\mid}{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{e}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{fix}}\,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau=e\mathrel{\mid}{e_{1}}\,{e_{2}}\mathrel{\mid}(e_{1},\,e_{2})\mathrel{\mid}\lambda x\mathrel{.}e\\
&&&\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{let}}\,x=e_{1}\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e_{2}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{match}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}\\
&&&\mathrel{\mid}\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e\mathrel{\mid}e[\tau]\mathrel{\mid}(e_{1},\,e_{2})\\
\mbox{Branch}&b&\mathrel{::=}&pat\mapsto e\\
\mbox{Pattern}&pat&\mathrel{::=}&x\mathrel{\mid}{C[\overrightarrow{\alpha}]}\,{pat}\mathrel{\mid}(pat_{1},\,pat_{2})\\
\mbox{Exp. Ctx.}&\Gamma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\\
\mbox{Type Ctx.}&\Delta&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Delta,\alpha\mathrel{\mid}\Delta,\tau_{1}\equiv\tau_{2}\end{array}

\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
contains polymorphism and GADTs, which makes it a good ersatz
OCaml that is still small and easy to reason about. GADTs are
particularly convenient, since they allow us to track invariants about
our objects in a similar fashion to dependent types. Compared to
Core-ML
,

\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
’s signatures now store type constants and constructors that are
parametrized by other types. We show the typing judgments for the
language in Fig.
9
. The term language is similar to

Core-ML
, with the addition of the usual terms for supporting abstraction
over types (i.e.
\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e
) and type applications (i.e.

e[\tau]
).

The language is type-checked with the two typing judgments, one for
terms and one for patterns:

•

\boxed{\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau}
: expression
e
is of type
\tau

in typing context
\Delta
and expression context
\Gamma
.

•

\boxed{\Delta_{o}\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}\Delta;\Gamma}
:
pat
is of
type
\tau
and binds type variables in
\Delta
and variables in

\Gamma
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau}:\mbox{$e$ is of type $\tau$ in typing context $\Delta$ and expression context $\Gamma$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{e}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}D[\overrightarrow{\tau}]\lx@proof@logical@and\Sigma(C)=\forall{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}\mathrel{.}\tau_{1}\to D[\overrightarrow{\alpha}]\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}[\overrightarrow{\tau}]\Delta\vdash\overrightarrow{\tau}\,{\texttt{wf}}\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{e_{1}}\,{e_{2}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{2}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e_{2}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash(e_{1},\,e_{2}){\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{2}\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\Gamma(x)=\tau\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{fix}}\,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau=e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\Delta;\Gamma,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\lambda x\mathrel{.}e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\Delta;\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{2}\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e[\overrightarrow{\tau}_{1}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau[\overrightarrow{\tau}_{1}]\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}\mathrel{.}{\tau}\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{\tau}_{1}\,{\texttt{wf}}\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\alpha}\mathrel{.}\tau\Delta,\alpha;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{let}}\,x=e_{1}\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e_{2}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\Delta;\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\vdash e_{2}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\par\quad\par\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{match}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta;\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\text{for all }i.\Delta;\Gamma\vdash b_{i}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\to\tau\par\\[6.99997pt]
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash pat\mapsto e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}_{1}\Delta^{\prime};\Gamma^{\prime}\Delta,\Delta^{\prime};\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{2}\par\\[6.99997pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Delta_{o}\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}\Delta;\Gamma}:\mbox{$pat$ is of type $\tau$ and binds variables in $\Delta$ and $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
\Delta_{0}\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}\cdot;x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\par\\[6.99997pt]
\Delta_{0}\vdash(pat_{1},\,pat_{2}){\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta_{0}\vdash pat_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\downarrow}\Delta_{1};\Gamma_{1}\Delta_{0}\vdash pat_{2}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\downarrow}\Delta_{2};\Gamma_{2}\par\\[6.99997pt]
\Delta_{o}\vdash{C[\overrightarrow{\alpha}]}\,{p}at{\,\mathrel{:}\,}D[\overrightarrow{\tau_{2}}]\mathrel{\downarrow}\overrightarrow{\alpha},\overrightarrow{\tau_{1}}\equiv\overrightarrow{\tau_{2}},\Delta;\Gamma\lx@proof@logical@and\Sigma(C)=\forall{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}\mathrel{.}\tau\to D[\overrightarrow{\tau_{1}}]\Delta_{0},\overrightarrow{\alpha},\overrightarrow{\tau_{1}}\equiv\overrightarrow{\tau_{2}}\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}\Delta;\Gamma\end{array}

Figure 9:
The Typing of
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}

The expressive power of pattern matching is greatly enhanced by the
presence of a limited form of dependent types (i.e. types that depend
on other types).

Of particular interest are the type equalities introduced in the type
context in the
gp-con
rule. Let’s consider an example to
understand this (a complete discussion is fully developed
by
Xi et al. 2003
). As an example, we define the type of vectors
as lists indexed by their length (i.e. the classical dependent types
example).

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma&=&{\texttt{z}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*,{\texttt{s}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*\to*,{\texttt{vec}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,\\
&&{\texttt{Nil}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\beta}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{vec}}[{\texttt{z}},\beta],\\
&&{\texttt{Cons}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\alpha,\beta}\mathrel{.}(\beta\times{\texttt{vec}}[\alpha,\beta])\to{\texttt{vec}}[{\texttt{s}}[\alpha],\beta]\\
\end{array}

In the signature we need to define the type level encoding of natural
numbers: types
z
and
s
, and the type of vectors
vec
that
are indexed by their length and the type of their elements. Finally,
we define the constructor
Nil
of empty vectors of length zero,
thus the first index is the type
z
. And finally the constructor

Cons
that puts together an element of type
\beta
and a vector
of length
\alpha
to form a vector of length
\alpha+1
, that is

{\texttt{s}}[\alpha]
in
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
.

Vectors are interesting because they make many functions type safe by
relying on the more expressive type system. As an example, in
Figure
10
shows the function
zip
that joins two
vectors of the same length and produces a vector of the same lengths
containing pairs of values taken from the original vectors:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\texttt{fix}}\,{\texttt{zip}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}\mathrel{.}\\
{\quad({\texttt{vec}}[\alpha,\beta_{1}]\times{\texttt{vec}}[\alpha,\beta_{2}])\to{\texttt{vec}}[\alpha,(\beta_{1},\,\beta_{2})]}=\\[1.99997pt]
\Lambda\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\mathrel{.}{\lambda v\mathrel{.}{{\texttt{match}}\,v\,{\texttt{with}}\ }}\\[1.99997pt]
|\ ({\texttt{Nil}}[\beta_{1}],{\texttt{Nil}}[\beta_{2}])\mapsto{\texttt{Nil}}[\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}]\\[1.99997pt]
|\ ({\texttt{Cons}}[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}](x,\,xs),{\texttt{Cons}}[\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}](y,\,ys)\mapsto\\[1.99997pt]
\quad{\texttt{Cons}}[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}]((x,\,y),\,{{\texttt{zip}}[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]{(xs,\,ys)}}\,{)}\end{array}

Figure 10:
Zip in
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}

In this case, type refinement happens for example in the second
branch, where in the body we have the choice of referring to the
length as
\alpha_{1}
or
\alpha_{2}
, knowing they have to be the same.
In fact, when typing the pattern in rule
gp-con
context
\Delta

is extended with
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{2}

from which is easy to conclude that
\alpha_{1}\equiv\alpha_{2}
.
Additionally, the type constraint that the vectors are of the same
length makes it obvious that the diagonal cases (those when one vector
has more elements than the other) are impossible.

Since
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
has strong type separation the operational semantics, in
Figure
11
, is similar to the semantics for
Core-ML
, after
all, type information is irrelevant at run-time. The interested reader
can find the meta-theory in
(
Xi et al. 2003
)
.
We define values and environments as:

\begin{array}[]{lrcll}\mbox{Values}&v&\mathrel{::=}&{{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{v}}\,{\mathrel{\mid}}(v_{1},\,v_{2})\mathrel{\mid}(\lambda x\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\mathrel{\mid}(\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\\
\mbox{Environments}&\theta;\rho&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot;\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\theta;(\rho,v/x)\mathrel{\mid}(\theta,\tau/\alpha);\rho\\
\end{array}

And with them the semantics are implemented with two judgments, one to
evaluate expressions and the other to compute matching, the matching
operation simply augments the environment inside of the branches in
case expressions:

•

\boxed{e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v}
: Expression
e
evaluates to value
v
in environment
\rho
.

•

\boxed{pat\doteq v\backslash\theta;\rho}
: Value
v
matches pattern
pat
and produces env.
\theta;\rho
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v}:\mbox{Expression $e$ evaluates to value $v$ in envirnoment $\rho$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
({e_{1}}\,{e_{2}})[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e_{1}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow(\lambda x\mathrel{.}e)[\theta^{\prime};\rho^{\prime}]e_{2}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v^{\prime}e[\theta^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},v^{\prime}/x]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
e[\tau][\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}{e^{\prime}}e^{\prime}[\theta,\tau/\alpha;\rho]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
x[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\rho(x)=v\par\quad\par({\texttt{let}}\,x=e_{1}\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e_{2})[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e_{1}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}e_{2}[\theta;\rho,v_{1}/x]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
({\texttt{match}}\,e\,{\texttt{with}}\ {\mid pat_{1}\mapsto e_{b}}\mathrel{::}\overrightarrow{b})[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\lx@proof@logical@and e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}{pat_{1}}\doteq v\backslash\theta^{\prime};\rho^{\prime}e_{b}[\theta,\theta^{\prime};\rho,\rho^{\prime}]\Downarrow v\par\\[10.00002pt]
{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{e}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{v}e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v\par\quad\par(\lambda x\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow(\lambda x\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\par\\[10.00002pt]
(\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow(\Lambda\alpha\mathrel{.}e)[\theta;\rho]\par\\[10.00002pt]
(e_{1},\,e_{2})[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow(v_{1},\,v_{2})\lx@proof@logical@and e_{1}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v_{1}e_{2}[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow v_{2}\par\quad\par{\texttt{fix}}\,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t=e[\theta;\rho]\Downarrow ve[\theta;(\rho,{\texttt{fix}}\,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t=e/f)]\Downarrow v\par\\[6.99997pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{pat\doteq v\backslash\theta;\rho}:\mbox{Value $v$ matches pattern $pat$ and produces env. $\theta;\rho$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[5.0pt]
x\doteq v\backslash\cdot;(v/x)\par\quad\par((pat_{1},\,pat_{2})\doteq(v_{1},\,v_{2})\backslash\theta_{1},\theta_{2};\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\lx@proof@logical@and pat_{1}\doteq v_{1}\backslash\theta_{1};\rho_{1}pat_{2}\doteq v_{2}\backslash\theta_{2};\rho_{2}\par\\[10.00002pt]
{C[\overrightarrow{\alpha}]}\,{pat}\doteq{C[\overrightarrow{\tau}]}\,{v}\backslash(\overrightarrow{\tau}/\overrightarrow{\alpha},\theta);\rho v\doteq pat/\theta;\rho\par\end{array}

Figure 11:
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
Big-Step Operational Semantics

7
Deep Embedding of SF into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}

We now show how to translate objects and types defined in the
syntactic framework SF into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
using a deep embedding. We take
advantage of the advanced features of
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
’s type system to fully
type-check the result. Our representation of SF objects and types is
inspired by
(
Benton et al. 2012b
)
but uses GADTs instead of full dependent
types. We add the idea of typed context shifts instead of renamings to
represent weakening. This is necessary to be able to completely erase
types at run-time.

To ensure SF terms are well-scoped and well-typed, we define SF types
in
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
and index their representations by their type and
context. The following types are only used as indices for
GADTs. Because of that, they do not have any term constructors.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma&=&{\texttt{base}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*\to*,{\texttt{arr}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,{\texttt{boxed}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*\to*,\\
&&{\texttt{prod}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,{\texttt{unit}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*\\
\end{array}

We define three type families, one for each of SF’s type
constructors. It is important to note the number of type parameters
they require. Base types take one parameter: a type from the
signature. Function types simply have an input and output
type. Finally, boxes contain just one type.

Terms are also indexed by the contexts in which they are valid. To
this effect, we define two types to statically represent contexts.
Analogously to the representation of types, these two types are only
used during type-checking and there will be no instances at run-time.
The type
nil
represents an empty context and thus has no
parameters. And the constructor
cons
has two parameters the first
one is the rest of the context and the second one is the type of the
top-most variable so far.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma&=&\dots,{\texttt{nil}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*,{\texttt{cons}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*\\
\end{array}

We show the the encoding well-typed SF objects and
types in Fig.
12
. Every declaration is parametrized with the type of constructors
that the user defined inside of the
@@@signature

blocks.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma&=&\dots,{\texttt{var}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Top}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\alpha}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{var}}[{\texttt{cons}}[\gamma,\alpha],\alpha],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Pop}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\alpha,\beta}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{var}}[\gamma,\alpha]\to{\texttt{var}}[{\texttt{cons}}[\gamma,\beta],\alpha],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{sftm}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,{\texttt{sp}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*,*)\to*\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Lam}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\alpha,\tau}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{sftm}}[{\texttt{cons}}[\gamma,{\texttt{base}}[\alpha]],\tau]\to{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,{\texttt{arr}}[{\texttt{base}}[\alpha],\tau]],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Var}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\alpha}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{var}}[\gamma,\alpha]\to{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,{\texttt{base}}[\alpha]],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Box}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\tau}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{sftm}}[\cdot,\tau]\to{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,\tau],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{C}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\tau,\alpha}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{con}}[\tau,\alpha]\times{\texttt{sp}}[\gamma,\tau]\to{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,{\texttt{base}}[\alpha]],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Empty}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\tau}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{sp}}[\gamma,\tau,\tau],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Cons}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3}}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,\tau_{1}]\times{\texttt{sp}}[\gamma,\tau_{2},\tau_{3}]\to{\texttt{sp}}[\gamma,{\texttt{arr}}[\tau_{1},\tau 2],\tau_{3}],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{shift}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Id}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{shift}}[\gamma,\gamma],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Suc}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\delta,\alpha}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{shift}}[\gamma,\delta]\to{\texttt{shift}}[{\texttt{cons}}[\gamma,{\texttt{base}}[\alpha]],\delta],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{sub}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*,\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Shift}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\delta}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{shift}}[\gamma,\delta]\to{\texttt{sub}}[\gamma,\delta],\\[1.7pt]
&&{\texttt{Dot}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\forall{\gamma,\delta,\tau}\mathrel{.}{\texttt{sub}}[\gamma,\delta]\times{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,\tau]\to{\texttt{sub}}[\gamma,{\texttt{cons}}[\delta,\tau]]\\[1.7pt]
\end{array}

Figure 12:
Syntactic Framework Definition

The specification takes the form of the type

{\texttt{con}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(*,*)\to*
, where
con
is the name of a
constructor from the signature indexed by the type of its parameters
and the base type they produce. So, all the SF definitions the user
makes add constructors, in our closure conversion example some
constructors could be for instance:
capp
,

clam
,
clo
.

Variables and terms are indexed by two types, the first parameter is
always their context and the second is their type. The type
var

represents variables with two constructors:
Top
represents the
variable that was introduced last in the context and if
Top

corresponds to the de Bruijn index 0 then the constructor
Pop

represents the successor of the variable that it takes as parameter.
It is interesting to consider the parameters of these constructors.

Top
is simply indexed by its context and type (variables
\gamma

and
\alpha
respectively). On the other hand,
Pop
requires three
type parameter: the first
\gamma
represents a context,
\alpha
the
resulting type of the variable, and
\beta
the type of the extension
of the context. These parameter make it so that if we apply the
constructor
Pop
to a variable of type
\alpha
in context
\gamma

we obtain a variable of type
\alpha
in the context
\gamma
extended
with type
\beta
.
Top
and
Pop
contexts cannot
be empty because they refer to existing variables. The constructor

Top
is indexed by a context with at least one variable,
and its type is the same as the top variable. On the other hand, the
constructor
Pop
is also indexed by a non-empty context but
its type corresponds to the type of the variable that it takes as a
parameter.

As mentioned, terms described by the type family
sftm
are indexed by
their context and their type. It is interesting to check in some
detail how the indices of the term constructors follow the typing
rules from Fig.
6
. The constructor for lambda terms
(
Lam
), extends the context
\gamma
with base type
\alpha
and
then it produces a term in
\gamma
of function type from the base
type
\alpha
to the type of the body
\tau
. The constructor for
boxes simply forces its body to be closed by using the context type

nil
. The constructor
Var
simply embeds variables as terms.
Finally the
C
constructor has two parameters, one is the name of
the constructor from the user’s definitions that constrains the type
of the second parameter, the other is the term of the appropriated
type.

The definition of substitution is a modified presentation of the
substitution for well-scoped de Bruijn indices, as for example
presented in
(
Benton et al. 2012b
)
. We define two types,
sub
and

shift
indexed by two contexts, the domain and the range of the
substitutions. Substitutions are either a shift (constructor

Shift
) or the combination of a term for the top-most variable and
the rest of the substitution (constructor
Dot
).

Our implementation differs from
Benton et al. 2012b
in the
representation of renamings. Benton et.al define substitutions and
renamings, the latter as a way of representing shifts. However to
compute a shift, they need the context that they use to index the
data-types. Hence, contexts are not erasable during run-time. As we do
want contexts to be erasable at run-time, we cannot use
renamings. Instead, we replace renamings with typed shifts (defined in
type
shift
), that encode how many variables we are shifting
over. This is encoded in the indices of shifts.

Variables that use de Bruijn indices and shifts ultimately correspond
to natural numbers, as they encode how many binders we need to
traverse, or how many variables a shift adds. However, we use
specialized data types because we explicitly carry the typing
information in the indices. Finally, we omit the function implementing
the substituti0on as it is standard. We will simply mention that we
implement a function
apply_sub
of type:

\forall{\gamma,\delta,\tau}\mathrel{.}{{\texttt{sftm}}[\gamma,\tau]\to{\texttt{sub}}[\gamma,\delta]\to{\texttt{sftm}}[\delta,\tau]}

That applies a substitution moving a term from context
\gamma
to
context
\delta
.

The finding the path to a variable example from
Section
1
, contains a signature that defines the untyped

\lambda
-calculus. This would result in a new type, and two new
constructors for the existing type
con
. We will prefix the
definitions with
def_
to create unique names:

•

A new type:
{\texttt{def\_tm}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}*

•

A constructor for applications:

{\texttt{def\_app}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{con}}[{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}],{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}]\times{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}]]

•

A constructor for abstractions:

{\texttt{def\_lam}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{con}}[{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}],{\texttt{arr}}[{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}],{\texttt{base}}[{\texttt{def\_tm}}]]]

To implement the signature we declare a new type for the terms of the
defined language (
def_tm
), and we extend the type of SF constructors, with as
many constructors as our signature had (
def_app
and

def_lam
).

8
From
Core-ML
with Contextual Types to
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}

In this section, we translate
Core-ML
with contextual types into the
lower level
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
. Because our embedding of the syntactic framework SF
in
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
is intrinsically typed, there is no need to extend the
type-checker to accommodate contextual objects. Further, recall that
we restricted quoted variables and parameter variables such that the
matching operation remains first order. In addition, as our deep
embedding uses a representation with canonical names (namely de Bruijn indices), we are able to translate pattern matching into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
’s
pattern matching; thus there is no need to extend the operational
semantics of the language.

The translation we describe in this section provides the footprint of
an implementation of it to directly generate OCaml code, as
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
is
essentially a subset of OCaml. It therefore shows how to extend a
functional programming language such as OCaml with the syntactic
framework with minimal impact on OCaml’s compiler.

Our main translation of
Core-ML
to
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
uses the following main operations:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\ulcorner\tau\urcorner,\ulcorner\Xi\urcorner,\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner:&\mbox{Translate types, signatures and contexts.}\\
\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}:&\mbox{Type directed translation of expressions.}\\
\ulcorner pat\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}:&\mbox{Translates patterns and outputs $\Gamma^{\prime}$ the}\\
&\mbox{context of the bound variables.}\\
\end{array}

We begin by translating SF types and contexts into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
types. These
types are used to index terms in the implementation of SF:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{SF Types:}&\ulcorner\textbf{a}\to A\urcorner&=&{\texttt{arr}}[\textbf{a},\ulcorner A\urcorner]\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\square\,A\urcorner&=&{\texttt{boxed}}[\ulcorner A\urcorner]\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\textbf{a}\urcorner&=&\textbf{a}\\[6.99997pt]
\mbox{SF Contexts:}&\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner&=&{\texttt{nil}}[]\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\urcorner&=&{\texttt{cons}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]\\[1.99997pt]
\end{array}

The translation of SF terms is directed by their contextual type

\Psi\vdash A
, because it needs the context to perform the
translation of names to de Bruijn indices and the types to
appropriately index the terms.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{SF Terms:}&\ulcorner\lambda\,{x}.M\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}\to A}&=&{\texttt{Lam}}[{\texttt{cons}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}],\ulcorner A\urcorner]\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\Psi,\textbf{a}\vdash A}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\{M\}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\square\,A}&=&{\texttt{Box}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\ulcorner A\urcorner]{\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\cdot\vdash A}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}&=&{\texttt{Var}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]{\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi}^{v}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}&=&{\texttt{C}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\ulcorner A\urcorner,\textbf{a}]{(\textbf{c},\ulcorner\overrightarrow{M}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A\downarrow\textbf{a}})}\\
&&&\text{with }\Sigma(\textbf{c})=A\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner M[\sigma]\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}&=&{\texttt{apply\_sub}}\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\Phi\vdash A}\ulcorner\sigma\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\Phi}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{'u}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}&=&u\\[3.99994pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{\#v}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}&=&{\texttt{Var}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]{\ulcorner{\texttt{v}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{N}\,{\overrightarrow{M}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A\to B\downarrow\textbf{a}}&=&{\texttt{Cons}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,{\texttt{arr}}[\ulcorner A\urcorner,\ulcorner B\urcorner],\textbf{a}]\\
&&&~~~{(\ulcorner N\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A},\ulcorner\overrightarrow{M}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash B\downarrow\textbf{a}})}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}\downarrow\textbf{a}}&=&{\texttt{Empty}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a},\textbf{a}]\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{SF Vars.:}&\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\vdash\textbf{a}}^{v}&=&{\texttt{Top}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner y\urcorner_{\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{b}\vdash\textbf{a}}^{v}&=&{\texttt{Pop}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a},\textbf{b}]\ulcorner y\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}^{v}\\[1.99997pt]
\mbox{Param. Vars:}&\ulcorner{\texttt{x}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}&=&x\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{\#v}}\urcorner_{\Psi,y{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}^{\prime}\vdash\textbf{a}}&=&{{\texttt{Pop}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}]\ulcorner{\texttt{v}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}}\\[1.99997pt]
\end{array}

There are are three kinds of variables in the syntactic framework SF:
bound variables, quoted variables and parameter variables. Each kind
requires a different translation strategy. Bound variables are
translated into de Bruijn indices where the numbers are encoded using
the constructors
Top
and
Pop
. Quoted variables are simply
translated into the
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
variables they quote. And finally the
parameter variables are translated into a
Var
constructor to
indicate that the resulting expression is an SF variable, and the
shifts (indicated by extra ’
#
’) are translated to applications of
the constructor
Pop
.

Notice how substitutions are not part of the representation. They are
translated to the eager application of
apply_sub
, an OCaml
function that performs the substitution. Before we call

apply_sub
we translate the substitution. This amounts to
generating the right shift for empty substitutions and otherwise
recursively translating the terms and the substitution.

We also need to translate SF patterns into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
expressions with the
right structure. The special cases are:

•

Variables are translated to de Bruijn indexes.

•

Quoted variables simply translate to
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
variables.

•

Parameter variables translate to a
pattern that matches only variables by specifying the

Var
constructor.

The translation of patterns follows the same line as the translation
of terms, however, we do not use the indices of type variables in

\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
patterns. This is indicated by writing an underscore.

Additionally, the translation of substitutions amounts to generating the right shift for
empty substitutions and otherwise recursively translating the terms and the substitution:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner_{\Psi}^{\Psi}&=&{\texttt{Shift}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner]({\texttt{Id}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner])\\[1.99997pt]
\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner_{\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}}^{\Phi}&=&{\texttt{Shift}}[\ulcorner\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\urcorner,\ulcorner\Phi\urcorner]\\
&&\quad({\texttt{Suc}}[\ulcorner\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\urcorner,\ulcorner\Phi\urcorner]{\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner_{\Psi}^{\Phi}})\\[1.99997pt]
\ulcorner\sigma,x/M\urcorner_{\Psi}^{\Phi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A}&=&{\texttt{Dot}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\ulcorner\Phi\urcorner,\textbf{a}](\ulcorner\sigma\urcorner_{\Psi}^{\Phi},\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\Phi\vdash\textbf{a}})\\[1.99997pt]
\end{array}

Our Babybel prototype (see also our examples from the beginning)
exploits the power GADTs to model context relations and relies on
OCaml’s type reconstruction engine
(
Garrigue and Rémy 2013
)
to infer the
omitted indices.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\mbox{SF Patterns:}&\ulcorner\lambda\,{x}.R\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}\to A}^{\Gamma}&=&{\texttt{Lam}}[\_,\_,\_]\ulcorner R\urcorner_{\Psi,\textbf{a}\vdash A}^{\Gamma}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\{R\}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\square\,A}^{\Gamma}&=&{\texttt{Box}}[\_,\_]{\ulcorner R\urcorner_{\cdot\vdash A}^{\Gamma}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}^{\cdot}&=&{\texttt{Var}}[\_,\_]{\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi}^{p}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\textbf{c}}\,{\overrightarrow{R}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}^{\Gamma}&=&{\texttt{C}}[\_,\_,\_]{\ulcorner\overrightarrow{R}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A\downarrow\textbf{a}}^{\Gamma}}\\
&&&\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with }\Sigma(\textbf{c})=A\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{'u}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}^{u{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\Psi\vdash A\urcorner}&=&u\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{\#x}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}}^{x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\Psi\vdash A\urcorner}&=&{\texttt{Var}}[\_,\_]{x}\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{\texttt{\#\#x}}\urcorner_{\Psi,y{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\_\vdash\textbf{a}}^{x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\Psi,y{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\_\vdash A\urcorner}&=&{\texttt{Var}}[\_,\_]({{\texttt{Pop}}[\_,\_,\_]x})\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner{R}\,{\overrightarrow{R^{\prime}}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A\to B\downarrow\textbf{a}}^{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}&=&{\texttt{Cons}}[\_,\_,\_,\_](\ulcorner R\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}^{\Gamma},\ulcorner\overrightarrow{R^{\prime}}\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash B\downarrow\textbf{a}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}\downarrow\textbf{a}}^{\cdot}&=&{\texttt{Empty}}[\_,\_]\\[6.99997pt]
\mbox{SF Variables:}&\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}}^{p}&=&{\texttt{Top}}[\_,\_]\\[1.99997pt]
&\ulcorner y\urcorner_{\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{b}}^{p}&=&{\texttt{Pop}}[\_,\_,\_]{\ulcorner y\urcorner_{\Psi}^{p}}\\[1.99997pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating Types:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner D\urcorner&=&D[]\\
\ulcorner\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\urcorner&=&\ulcorner\tau_{1}\urcorner\to\ulcorner\tau_{2}\urcorner\\
\ulcorner[\Psi\vdash A]\urcorner&=&\ulcorner A\urcorner\\
\ulcorner\overrightarrow{\tau}\urcorner&=&\ulcorner\tau_{1}\urcorner,\dots,\ulcorner\tau_{n}\urcorner\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating Signatures:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner&=&\cdot\\

\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner&=&\cdot\\

\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating SF Contexts:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner&=&{\texttt{nil}}[]\\
\ulcorner\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\textbf{a}\urcorner&=&{\texttt{cons}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]\\
\end{array}

The translation of
