\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating expressions:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}&=&x\\
\ulcorner{C}\,{\overrightarrow{e}}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash D}&=&{C[~]}\,{\ulcorner\overrightarrow{e}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{\tau}}}\text{ with }\Xi(C)=\overrightarrow{\tau}\to D\\
\ulcorner{\texttt{fun}}\,f(x)=e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}}&=&{\texttt{fix}}\,f{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}\urcorner=\lambda x\mathrel{.}{\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\vdash\tau_{2}}}\\
\ulcorner{i}\,{e}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}&=&{\ulcorner i\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\to\tau}}\,{\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}}}\\
&&\text{with }\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau_{1}\to\tau\\
\ulcorner{\texttt{let}}\,x=i\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}&=&{\texttt{let}}\,x=\ulcorner i\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}}\mathrel{{\texttt{in}}}\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau_{1}\vdash\tau}\\
&&\text{with }\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau_{1}\\
\ulcorner{\texttt{match}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{b}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}&=&{\texttt{match}}\,\ulcorner i\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}}\,{\texttt{with}}\ \ulcorner\overrightarrow{b}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\to\tau}\\
&&\text{with }\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau_{1}\\
\ulcorner e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{\tau}}&=&\ulcorner e_{1}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}},\dots,\ulcorner e_{n}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{n}}\\
\ulcorner[\hat{\Psi}\vdash M]\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash[\Psi\vdash A]}&=&\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}\\
\ulcorner{\texttt{cmatch}}\,i\,{\texttt{with}}\ \overrightarrow{c}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}&=&{\texttt{match}}\,\ulcorner i\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash[\Psi\vdash A]}\,{\texttt{with}}\ \ulcorner\overrightarrow{c}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash[\Psi\vdash A]\to\tau}\\
&&\text{with }\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}[\Psi\vdash A]\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating branches and patterns:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner pat\mapsto e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\to\tau_{2}}&=&\ulcorner pat\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mapsto\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau_{2}}\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner x\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}^{x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau}&=&x\\[1.99997pt]
\ulcorner{C}\,{\overrightarrow{pat}}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash D}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}&=&{C[]}\,{\ulcorner\overrightarrow{pat}\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{\tau}}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\\[1.99997pt]
&&\hskip 28.45274pt\text{with }\Xi(C)=\overrightarrow{\tau}\to D\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\text{Translating branches:}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\ulcorner[\Psi\vdash R]\mapsto e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash[\Psi\vdash A]\to\tau}&=&\ulcorner R\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mapsto\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau}\\
\end{array}

Figure 14:
Translating Computational Expressions

Finally we show that the translation from
Core-ML
with contextual types into
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
preserves types.

Theorem 8.1

(Main)
.

1.

If
\Gamma\vdash e\mathrel{\Leftarrow}\tau
then
\cdot;\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner\vdash\ulcorner e\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\tau\urcorner
.

2.

If
\Gamma\vdash i\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\tau
then
\cdot;\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner\vdash\ulcorner i\urcorner_{\Gamma\vdash\tau}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\tau\urcorner
.

Our result follows form mutual induction on the typing derivations and
relies on several lemmas that deal with the other judgments and
context lookups. Appendix
7
contains the proofs.

Lemma 2

(Ambient Context)
.

If
\Gamma({\texttt{u}})=[\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}]
then

\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner(u)={\texttt{sftm}}[\ulcorner\Psi\urcorner,\textbf{a}]
.

Lemma 3

(Terms)
.

1.

If
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A
then
\cdot;\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner\vdash\ulcorner M\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\Psi\vdash A\urcorner
.

2.

If
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi
then
\cdot;\ulcorner\Gamma\urcorner\vdash\ulcorner\sigma\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash\Phi}:\ulcorner\Psi\vdash\Phi\urcorner

Lemma 4

(Pat.)
.

If
\vdash pat{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\tau\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma
then
\cdot\vdash\ulcorner pat\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}^{\Gamma}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\tau\urcorner\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 5

(Ctx. Pat.)
.

If
\Psi\vdash R{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A\mathrel{\downarrow}\Gamma
then
\cdot\vdash\ulcorner R\urcorner_{\Psi\vdash A}^{\Gamma}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\ulcorner\Psi\vdash A\urcorner\mathrel{\downarrow}{\Gamma}
.

Given our set-up, the proofs for the are straightforward by induction
on the structure of
\Gamma
and the typing derivation for
Lemma
4
,
5
,
6
.

9
A Proof of Concept Implementation

In this section, we describe the implementation
3
3

3

Available at

www.github.com/fferreira/babybel/
of Babybel which uses the
ideas described in this chapter. One major difference is that Babybel
translates OCaml programs that use syntax extensions for contextual SF
types and terms and translates them into pure OCaml with GADTs. In
fact, even our input OCaml programs may use GADTs to for example
describe context relations on SF contexts (see also our examples from
Sec.
2
).

The presence of GADTs in our source language also means that we can
specify precise types for functions where we can quantify over
contexts. Let’s revisit some of the types of the programs that we
wrote earlier in Sec.
2
:

•

rewrite
:

\gamma
.

[
\gamma

\vdash\,

tm
]
\rightarrow~
[
\gamma

\vdash\,

tm
]
:
we implicitly quantify over all contexts
\gamma

and then we take a potentially open term and return another term in
the same context. These constraints imposed in the types are due to
being able to index types with types thanks to GADTs.

•

get_path
:

\gamma
.

[
\gamma
,
x
:
tm

\vdash\,

tm
]
\rightarrow~
path
: In
this case we quantify over all contexts, but the input of the
function is some term in a non-empty context. On the left of the
turn-style our type mandates that there is at least one assumption
of type
tm
. Notice how the return type is not inside a
box(i.e.: square braces) because it is a regular OCaml recursive
type and not a contextual type. We can mix and match as necessary.

•

conv
:

\gamma

\delta
.(
\gamma
,

\delta
)

rel
\rightarrow~
[
\gamma

\vdash\,

tm
]
\rightarrow~
[
\delta

\vdash\,

ctm
]
:

This final example shows that we can also use the contexts to index
regular OCaml GADTs. In this function we are translating between
terms in different representations. Naturally, their contexts
contain assumptions of different type. To be able to translate
between these different context representations, it is necessary to
establish a relation between these contexts. So we need to define a
special OCaml type (i.e.:
rel
) that relates variable to
variable in each contexts.

By embedding the SF in OCaml using contextual types, we can combine and use the
impure features of OCaml. Our example, in Section
1
takes
advantage of them in our implementation of backtracking with exceptions. Additionally,
performing I/O or using references works seamlessly in the prototype.

The presence of GADTs in our target language also makes the actual
implementation of Babybel simpler than the theoretical description, as
we take advantage of OCaml’s built-in type reconstruction. In addition
to GADTs, our implementation depends on several OCaml extensions. We
use Attributes from Section 7.18 of the reference
manual
(
Leroy et al. 2016a
)
and strings to embed the specification of
our signature. We use quoted strings from Section 7.20 to implement
the boxes for terms (
{
t
|
.\hskip-0.71114pt.\hskip-0.71114pt.
.|
t
}
) and patterns
(
{
p
|
.\hskip-0.71114pt.\hskip-0.71114pt.
.|
p
}
). All these appear as annotations in the
internal Abstract Syntax Tree in the compiler implementation. To
perform the translation (based on Section
8
) we define a
PPX rewriter as discussed in Section 23.1 of the OCaml manual. In our
rewriter, we implement a parser for the framework SF and translate all
the annotations using our embedding.

The Babybel prototype has been used to implement several case studies
that expand from the examples at the beginning of this chapter. The
Babybel distribution contains the following case studies besides some
smaller programs used as a test suite.

•

The example that removes syntactic sugar from Section
1
.

•

The example that finds the path to a variable from Section
1
.

•

The closure conversion example from Section
1
.

•

Inferring types for MiniML.

•

An evaluator for an untyped MiniML.

•

Comparing
\lambda
-terms up to alpha renaming.

•

Translating the
\lambda
-calculus to a CPS form.

Currently, implementing these programs was straightforward and not
having to think about the representation of binders was liberating.
Along with the examples there are a couple of compiler phases, it would be
interesting to implement a small compiler combining them and
generating some code. Much remains to be done, but the prototype is
already usable
4
4

4

Given that type-checking is done by OCaml on
the translation, the error messages are far from perfect, but
otherwise it is easy to use.
.

10
Related Work

Babybel and the syntactic framework SF are derived from ideas that
originated in proof checkers based on the logical framework LF such as
the Twelf system
(
Pfenning and Schürmann 1999
)
. In the same category are the
proof and programming languages
Delphin
(
Poswolsky and Schürmann 2009
)
and
Beluga
(
Pientka and Cave 2015
)
that offer a computational language on
top of the LF. In many ways, this chapter and Babybel are a
distillation of Beluga’s ideas applied to a mainstream programming
language. As a consequence, we have shown that we can get some of the
benefits from Beluga at a much lower cost, since we do not have to
build a stand-alone system or extend the compiler of an existing
language to support contexts, contextual types and objects.

Our approach of embedding an LF specification language into a host
language is in spirit related to the systems Abella
(
Gacek et al. 2012
)

and Hybrid
(
Felty and Momigliano 2012
)
that use a two-level approach. In these
systems we embed the specification language (typically hereditary
harrop formulas) in first-order logic (or a variant of it). While our
approach is similar in spirit, we focus on embedding SF specifications
into a programming language instead of embedding it into a proof
theory. Moreover, our embedding is type preserving by construction.

There are also many approaches and tools that specifically add support
for writing programs that manipulate syntax with binders – even if
they do not necessarily use HOAS. FreshML
(
Shinwell et al. 2003
)
and
C
\alpha
ml
(
Pottier 2006
)
extend OCaml’s data types with the
ideas of names and binders from nominal logic
(
Pitts 2003
)
. In
these system, name generation is an effect, and if the user is not
careful variables may extrude their scopes. Purity can be enforced by
adding a proof system with a decision procedure that statically
guarantees that no variable escapes its scope
(
Pottier 2007
)
.
This adds some complexity to the system. We feel that Babybel’s
contextual types offer a simpler formalism to deal with bound
variables. On the other hand, Babybel’s approach does not try to
model variables that do not have lexical scope, like top-level
definitions. Another related language is Romeo
(
Stansifer and Wand 2014
)

that uses ideas from Pure FreshML to represent binders. Where our
system statically catches variables escaping their scope, the Romeo
system either throws a run time exception or uses an SMT solver to
prove the absence of scoping issues. The Hobbits for
Haskell
(
Westbrook et al. 2011
)
system is implemented in a similar way
to ours, using quasi-quoting but they use a different formalism based
on the concepts of names and freshness. In general, parametric HOAS
(PHOAS)
(
Chlipala 2008
)
reuses the extensional (as in black
box functions) function space of the language, while our approach
introduces a distinction between an intensional and extensional
function space. A particular approach is described
in
(
Washburn and Weirich 2008
)
where the authors propose a library
that uses catamorphisms to compute over a parametric HOAS
representation. This is a powerful approach but requires a different
way of implementing recursive functions.

The separate intensional function space from SF allows us to
model binding and supports pattern matching. The extentional function
space allows us to write recursive functions. Following essentially
work started by Despeyroux, Schuermann, and
Pfenning
(
Despeyroux et al. 1997
)
and then later in the work on Beluga, we
use a modality to embed syntactic objects characterized using the
intensional function space into our programs. This is done while
preserving the host language’s (ML) extensional function space that
allows for computation.

11
Conclusion

In this chapter, we describe the syntactic framework SF (a simply
typed variant of the logical framework LF with the necessity modality
from S4) and explain the embedding of SF into a functional programming
language using contextual types. This gives programmers the ability to
write programs that manipulate abstract syntax trees with binders
while knowing at type checking time that no variables extrude their
scope. We also show how to translate the extended language back into a
first order representation. For this, we use de Bruijn indices and
GADTs to implement the SF in
\text{Core-ML}^{\text{gadt}}
. Important characteristics of the
embedding are that it preserves the phase separation, making types
(and thus contexts) erasable at run-time. This allows pattern matching
to remain first-order and thus it is possible to compile with the
traditional algorithms.

Finally, we describe Babybel an implementation of these ideas that
embeds SF in OCaml using Contextual Types. The embedding is flexible
enough that we can take advantage of the more powerful type system in
OCaml to make the extension more powerful. We use GADTs in our
examples to express more powerful invariants (e.g. that the
translation preserves the context).

Chapter 4
Contextual Types and Type Theory

1
Introduction

This is the last chapter
1
1

1

This is the last chapter before the
concluding remarks and conclusion.
, but in a sense it is not the
end but the beginning of what we hope will be a fruitful research
path. This chapter presents the first steps on answering an important
question in this field, namely how to integrate contextual types and
the logical framework LF with a reasoning language that provides full
dependent types. Here, we develop a system that extends the ideas of
Chapter
3
to a system that combines the logical
framework LF
(
Harper et al. 1993
)
with an extended Martin-Löf style
type theory
(
Martin-Löf 1984
)

(MLTT). As in Babybel, the embedding uses contextual
types
(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)
to mediate between both layers and allow
the system to effortlessly represent open objects.

Furthermore, this system can be seen also as an extension of the
theory of Beluga
(
Pientka and Cave 2015
)
into full dependent types. The
resulting system supports the definition of abstract syntax with
binders and the use of substitutions. This simplifies proofs about
systems with binders by providing for free the substitution lemma and
lemmas about the equational theory of substitutions. As in Beluga, we
mediate between specifications and computations via contextual types.
However, unlike Beluga, we can embed and use computations directly in
contextual objects and types, hence we allow the arbitrary mixing of
specifications and computations. Moreover, dependent types allow for
reasoning about proofs in addition to reasoning about LF
specifications. This resulted in the Orca language, with its prototype
implementation available at
https://github.com/orca-lang/orca
.

From an expressivity perspective, Orca and Beluga differ as when using the
former the user can reason about specifications
and

computational functions. While the latter cannot reason about
computations and instead needs to specify the computations in a
relational style in LF.

The focus of this chapter is to describe the theory that is
implemented by the Orca language (where the prototype already
implements some extensions to the theory presented here) and to
describe some ideas for an appropriate surface language for this
theory. In particular, we will discuss how the prototype tries to
alleviate the mediation between the two main syntactic categories (the
specifications in LF and the reasoning language) by performing what we
call a box inference pass on the surface language. Much remains to be
done. As we discuss in the future work section a particularly
important next step is to work on the meta-theory of this language.
This will settle the answer to the question how to combine LF
specifications with dependent types, while this chapter just hints at
the answer.

2
Example: Translating Boolean Types

Let’s consider a simple example of translating between two languages
with a different representation of boolean types. Starting from a
simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with boolean type:

\begin{array}[]{rlcl}\text{Types}&t&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{bool}}\mathrel{\mid}t\to t^{\prime}\\
\text{Expressions}&e&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{tt}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{ff}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{if}}\,e\,{\texttt{then}}\,e^{\prime}\,{\texttt{else}}\,e^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mid}\lambda{x}^{t}.e\mathrel{\mid}{e}\,{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mid}x\\
\text{Context}&\Gamma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\end{array}

We want to translate into a language that lacks booleans, but instead
it has unit and sums:

\begin{array}[]{rlcl}\text{Types}&T&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{1}}\mathrel{\mid}T+T^{\prime}\mathrel{\mid}T\to T^{\prime}\\
\text{Expressions}&E&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{()}}\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{inl}}^{T}\,E\mathrel{\mid}{\texttt{inr}}^{T}\,E\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&({\texttt{case}}\,E\,{\texttt{inl}}\,x\mapsto E^{\prime}\,\mid{\texttt{inr}}\,x\mapsto E^{\prime\prime})\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lambda{x}^{T}.E\mathrel{\mid}{E}\,{E^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mid}x\\
\text{Context}&\Delta&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot\mathrel{\mid}\Delta,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\end{array}

The typing for both languages is as one would expect. For completeness,
Figure
1
contains the typing rules for the source
language and Figure
2
contains the typing for the target language.

The idea for the translation is that type
bool
from the source
language can be represented by type
{\texttt{1}}+{\texttt{1}}
in the
target language, and that there is a mechanical way to translate
expressions in a way that preserves types.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t}:\text{$e$ is of type $t$ in context $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{tt}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{bool}}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{ff}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{bool}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{if}}\,e\,{\texttt{then}}\,e^{\prime}\,{\texttt{else}}\,e^{\prime\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{bool}}\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\lambda{x}^{t}.e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\to t^{\prime}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t^{\prime}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash{e}\,{e^{\prime}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash e{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t^{\prime}\to t\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t^{\prime}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}tx{\,\mathrel{:}\,}t\in\Gamma\end{array}

Figure 1:
The Typing of the Source Language

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Delta\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}:\text{$E$ is of type $T$ in context $\Delta$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta\vdash{\texttt{()}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{1}}\par\quad\par\Delta\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}Tx{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\in\Delta\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta\vdash{\texttt{inl}}^{T}\,E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}+T\Delta\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\par\quad\par\Delta\vdash{\texttt{inr}}^{T}\,E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T+T^{\prime}\Delta\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta\vdash{\texttt{case}}\,E\,{\texttt{inl}}\,x\mapsto E^{\prime}\,\mid{\texttt{inr}}\,x\mapsto E^{\prime\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}+T^{\prime\prime}\Delta,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\vdash E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\Delta,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime\prime}\vdash E^{\prime\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Delta\vdash\lambda{x}^{T}.E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\to T^{\prime}\Delta,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\par\quad\par\Delta\vdash{E}\,{E^{\prime}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\lx@proof@logical@and\Delta\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\to T\Delta\vdash E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\end{array}

Figure 2:
The Typing of the Target Language

The translation of types is a function from types in the source
language to types in the target language and it is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket{{\texttt{bool}}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{1}}+{\texttt{1}}\\
\llbracket{t\to t^{\prime}}\rrbracket&=&\llbracket{t}\rrbracket\to\llbracket{t^{\prime}}\rrbracket\end{array}

Finally, the translation of expressions depends on the translation of
types and it is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket{{\texttt{tt}}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{inl}}^{{\texttt{unit}}}\,{\texttt{()}}\\
\llbracket{{\texttt{ff}}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{inr}}^{{\texttt{unit}}}\,{\texttt{()}}\\
\llbracket{{\texttt{if}}\,e\,{\texttt{then}}\,e^{\prime}\,{\texttt{else}}\,e^{\prime}}\rrbracket&=&{\texttt{case}}\,\llbracket{e}\rrbracket\,{\texttt{inl \_}}\,\mapsto\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket\,\mid{\texttt{inr \_}}\,\mapsto\llbracket{e^{\prime\prime}}\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{\lambda{x}^{t}.e}\rrbracket&=&\lambda{x}^{\llbracket{t}\rrbracket}.\llbracket{e}\rrbracket\\
\llbracket{{e}\,{e^{\prime}}}\rrbracket&=&{\llbracket{e}\rrbracket}\,{\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket}\\
\llbracket{x}\rrbracket&=&x\\
\end{array}

This translation can be implemented as a function that computes the
expression in the target language. Moreover, it would be interesting to
establish some properties of the translation. One such property is the
fact that the translation is type preserving. Notice, that because
both languages have different types, we say that the idea of type
preservation is
up-to
the notion of translated types.

Let’s consider how to formalize this in Orca in the most direct way.
This is as opposed to formalizing an existing proof. The idea is to
minimize the lemmas and complexity of the formalization. To this end,
let’s specify the source and the target languages in an intrinsically
typed way. That is, using dependent types in the logical framework LF
to easily restrict the expressions to those that are well-typed.

The target language is similar to the source language, and it is
represented in a analogous way. Notice, how we model the binders in
the branches of the case expression with the logical framework’s
function space.

The translation of types is represented with a function from source
types to target types. We use the keyword
def
to introduce
a top level function definition with pattern matching. It is
implemented as follows:

⬇

tran
-
tp

:

(
\vdash\,

-

)

\rightarrow~

(
\vdash\,

-

)

|

First notice the type specification after the colon in the first line
where the turn-styles specify that these are specification types and
that they are closed types (after all these two languages types have
no binders in them). Then the implementation of the function goes by
pattern matching on the argument and producing the appropriate target
type according to the definition. In Orca, definitions are elaborated
in a type directed way to allow the user to omit the boxes when
possible. This is explained further when we describe the prototype in
Section
5
and we show the result of the
reconstruction of boxes for the translation function in
Figure
17
.

We proceed similarly to implement the translation of expressions.
However, let’s consider the type for the translation function first. A
first idea could be:

⬇

This type indicates that given a closed source expression of type

st
we can produce a closed target expression whose type
will be the translation of type
st
. Notice how this is a
place we interleave specifications and computations as the resulting
expression’s type is the result of actually calling the function

tran
-
tp
on the source expression. In a system without
computation in types like Beluga, we need to implement this using a
relation and this requires a couple of lemmas to show that the
relation is actually a function. As a reference
Appendix
8
contains a Beluga implementation of this
example. However, this type is not strong enough as the recursive
calls may go under binders. Thus we need to be able to translate open
expressions. We need to generalize this type to arbitrary contexts and
add a context relation that expresses that the source context and
target context grow in unison. Context relations are required because
so far, Orca contexts can contain assumptions of any kind. For this we
define a binary relation on contexts as follows:

⬇

data

In this relation the

contexts are related, and the

if we have two related contexts we can extend both contexts with a
fresh related assumption.

⬇

in the resulting type. The computation proceeds
by pattern matching, in the patterns we write

Inaccessible patterns are a concept from Agda that simplifies
implementing pattern matching, their value is forced by the other
variables, for example in the pattern for lambda expressions

(

that the types of expressions are closed, we annotate

and

with the empty substitution (i.e.:

]
)
that prevents them from using variables from the context. We build
contextual types by combining a term with a context (e.g.:

\vdash\,

s
-

), for contextual terms we use a different
syntax to make it easier to disambiguate between types and terms,
contexts and terms are combined in the following way:

g

.

This is a simple example, but even in its simplicity it showcases the
power of the Orca language. Even if the Orca prototype does not offer many of
the features present in Beluga, this example shows a hint to what
is coming in the future. In the next section, we peek behind the
curtains into Orca’s type theory.

3
Orca’s Core Calculus

Orca’s core calculus is composed of two separate languages, a

reasoning
and
computation
language on one side and a

specification
language on the other side. We refer to the
former as the computation language when we want to emphasize that we
can write programs with it, and as the reasoning language when trying
to emphasize that we can write proofs with it.

The syntax for the reasoning language is a fully dependently typed
theory, extended with contextual types and terms (in red in the
syntax), it is exactly as one would expect a type theory to be. As is
the case with Agda
(
Norell 2007
)
and Idris
(
Brady 2013
)
,
we do not have recursion in the calculus, but we do top-level
recursive definitions that allow for defining inductive functions. We
say contextual objects are boxed specifications as a reference to the
contextual modality in contextual modal type
theory
(
Nanevski et al. 2008
)
. The type for contexts is a
simplification that avoids discussing classifiers for contexts (i.e.:
context schemas). This is a limitation of the current presentation of
the theory, and it makes discussing totality and coverage difficult.
In short, it would be necessary to add schemas to be able to describe
a coverage checking algorithm.

\begin{array}[]{rlcll}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Reasoning Language}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Terms}&E,S,T&\mathrel{::=}&{\texttt{set}}_{n}&\text{A universe of level $n$}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T&\text{A function type}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lambda x.E&\text{An abstraction}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&E\,E^{\prime}&\text{An application}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&x&\text{A variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\mathbf{c}&\text{A constant}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M}]&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A contextual term}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A contextual type}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&{\texttt{ctx}}&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}The type of contexts}\end{array}

Conversely, the specification language is a version of the logical
framework LF, but instead of meta-variables, it supports a direct
embedding of computations together with a substitution, to unbox the
result of computation into a specification object. Of course, the
computation needs to produce a result of specification type.

\begin{array}[]{rlcll}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Specification Language}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Kinds}&K&\mathrel{::=}&\star&\text{The base kind}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}K&\text{A kind family}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Families}&\alpha,\beta&\mathrel{::=}&\mathbf{a}&\text{A base type}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta&\text{A type family}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&{\alpha}\,{M}&\text{A family instance}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Objects}&M,N&\mathrel{::=}&\widehat{\lambda}x.M&\text{An abstraction}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&M\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}N&\text{An application}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\widehat{x}&\text{A variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\widehat{\mathbf{c}}&\text{A constant}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma}&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0} An unboxed computation}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Substitutions}&\sigma&\mathrel{::=}&{}^{\wedge}&\text{An empty substitution}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&{\texttt{id}}_{n}&\text{An identity substitution,}\\
&&&&\text{with weakening}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,M&\text{A substitution extension}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Context}&\Psi&\mathrel{::=}&\emptyset&\text{Empty context}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lfloor{x}\rfloor&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0} An unboxed context variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha&\text{A context extension}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Erased Context}&\hat{\Psi}&\mathrel{::=}&\emptyset&\text{An empty context}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lfloor{x}\rfloor&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0} An unboxed context variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\hat{\Psi},\,\widehat{x}&\text{A variable declaration}\end{array}

Finally, we define contexts for computational variables, that as in
Chapter
3
will be the meta-variables for incomplete
objects, and a signature to define types and constants for computation
terms and for the specification language.

\begin{array}[]{rlcll}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Signature and Computational Context}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Context}&\Gamma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot&\text{Empty context}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T&\text{An assumption}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Signature}&\Sigma&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot&\text{Empty signature}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\Sigma,\textbf{c}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T&\text{A comp. constant}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\Sigma,\textbf{a}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K&\text{A spec. type}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\sigma,\widehat{\mathbf{c}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha&\text{A spec. constant}\\
\end{array}

We present a Martin-Löf style type theory with an infinite hierarchy
of universes extended with contextual types
[\Psi\vdash\alpha]

which represent a specification type
\alpha
in an open context

\Psi
. Specification types are classified by a single universe

\star
, together with an intensional function space

(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta
and constants. Substitutions can be an empty
substitution
∧
which weakens closed objects, an identity
substitution
{\texttt{id}}_{n}
that weakens the context with
n
elements (we
write
{\texttt{id}}={\texttt{id}}_{0}
as syntactic sugar), or a substitution extended
with an object for a variable
\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,M
. Contexts are either
empty
\emptyset
or a context extended with a new assumption

\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}A
.

Type theory terms and specification objects are typed with two
different judgments
\Gamma\vdash E:T
and

\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M:\beta
, respectively. For instance, we type
the type theory functions and specifications functions in the
following way:

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\widehat{\lambda}x.M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta\Gamma;\Psi,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta\end{array}

The two function spaces differ by the contexts they act on and that
the computational function space is extensional (i.e.: we can compute
with these functions) while the specification function space is
intensional (i.e.: we can inspect these functions with pattern
matching) in the sense of
Pfenning 2001
. Type theory

\lambda
-abstractions introduce variables in the computational
context
\Gamma
while specification
\widehat{\lambda}
-abstractions
use the specification context
\Psi
. The
\beta
-rule for each

\lambda
-abstraction uses its own substitution operation for its
corresponding class of variables denoted respectively
\{\theta\}E

and
[\sigma]M
. The definition for the substitution operations is
presented in Figures
3
,
4
,

5
, and
6
. This is in
contrast to
\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma}
for the closure that embeds a
computation of boxed type into a specification and is defined by the
following introduction rule:

\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[\sigma]\alpha\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Phi}\vdash{\alpha}]

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Substitution in the reasoning language}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\{\theta\}}\,{{\texttt{set}}_{n}}&=&{\texttt{set}}_{n}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T}&=&(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\{\theta\}}\,{S})\mathrel{\to}{\{\theta;x\mathrel{:=}x\}}\,{T}\text{ for $x$ free in $\theta$}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\lambda x.E}&=&\lambda x.({\{\theta;x\mathrel{:=}x\}}\,{E})\text{ for $x$ free in $\theta$}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{E\,E^{\prime}}&=&({\{\theta\}}\,{E})\,({\{\theta\}}\,{E^{\prime}})\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{x}&=&E\text{ if }x\mathrel{:=}E\text{ is in $\theta$}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{x}&=&x\text{ if }x\text{ is not in $\theta$}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\mathbf{c}}&=&\mathbf{c}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M}]}&=&[{{\{\theta\}}\,{\hat{\Psi}}}\mathrel{\triangleright}{{\{\theta\}}\,{M}}]\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]}&=&[{{\{\theta\}}\,{\Psi}}\vdash{{\{\theta\}}\,{\alpha}}]\end{array}

Figure 3:
Computation Substitution

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Substitution in objects of the specification language}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\{\theta\}}\,{\widehat{\lambda}x.M}&=&\widehat{\lambda}x.{({\{\theta\}}\,{M})}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{M\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}N}&=&({\{\theta\}}\,{M})\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}({\{\theta\}}\,{N})\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\widehat{x}}&=&\widehat{x}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}&=&\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma}}&=&\lfloor{({\{\theta\}}\,{E})}\rfloor_{({\{\theta\}}\,{\sigma})}\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Substitution in families of the specification language}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\{\theta\}}\,{\mathbf{a}}&=&\mathbf{a}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta}&=&(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}({\{\theta\}}\,{\alpha}))\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}({\{\theta\}}\,{\beta})\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{{\alpha}\,{M}}&=&{({\{\theta\}}\,{\alpha})}\,{({\{\theta\}}\,{M})}\\
\end{array}

Figure 4:
Computation Substitution in Specifications

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Substitution in the specification contexts}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\{\theta\}}\,{\emptyset}&=&\emptyset\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha}&=&({\{\theta\}}\,{\Psi}),\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}({\{\theta\}}\,{\alpha})\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Substitution in the specification substitutions}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{\{\theta\}}\,{{}^{\wedge}}&=&{}^{\wedge}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{{\texttt{id}}_{n}}&=&{\texttt{id}}_{n}\\
{\{\theta\}}\,{\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,M}&=&({\{\theta\}}\,{\sigma});\widehat{x}:=\,({\{\theta\}}\,{M})\\
\end{array}

Figure 5:
Computation Substitution in Contexts and Substitutions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{LF Substitution in objects}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{[\sigma]}\,{\widehat{\lambda}x.M}&=&\widehat{\lambda}x.{({[\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,\widehat{x}]}\,{M})}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{M\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}N}&=&({[\sigma]}\,{M})\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}({[\sigma]}\,{N})\\
{[\sigma]}\,{\widehat{x}}&=&M\quad\quad\text{ if $\widehat{x}\mathrel{:=}M$ is in $\sigma$}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{\widehat{x}}&=&\widehat{x}\quad\quad\text{ if $\widehat{x}$ is not in $\sigma$}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{\widehat{\mathbf{c}}}&=&\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}}}&=&\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{({[\sigma]}\,{\sigma^{\prime}})}\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{LF Substitution in families}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{[\sigma]}\,{\mathbf{a}}&=&\mathbf{a}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta}&=&(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}({[\sigma]}\,{\alpha}))\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}({[\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,\widehat{x}]}\,{\beta})\text{ with $\widehat{x}$ fresh in $\sigma$}\\
{[\sigma]}\,{{\alpha}\,{M}}&=&{({[\sigma]}\,{\alpha})}\,{({[\sigma]}\,{M})}\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{LF Substitution in kinds}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
{[\sigma]}\,{\star}&=&\star\\
{[\sigma]}\,{(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}K}&=&(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{[\sigma]}\,{\alpha})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}({[\sigma;\widehat{x}:=\,\widehat{x}]}\,{K})\text{ with $\widehat{x}$ fresh in $\sigma$}\\
\end{array}

Figure 6:
Specification Substitution

In addition to the description of the term calculus we present a
prototype implementation
2
2

2

Available
at:
http://github.com/orca-lang/orca
for our type theory which
supplements the calculus with recursive functions and Agda-style
dependent pattern matching
(
Norell 2007
)
extended to allow
matching on specifications including specification-level

\lambda
-abstractions and thus abstracting over binders.

In conclusion, we present a theory that allows for embedding
contextual LF specifications into a fully dependently typed language
that simplifies proofs about structures with syntactic binders (such
as programming languages and logics). Moreover, we have a prototype,
the Orca system, in which we implement some example proofs.

1
The Typing of Programs

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$E$ is of type $T$ in context $\Gamma$}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\texttt{set}}_{n}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{(n+1)}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}Tx{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\in\Gamma\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{c}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\mathbf{c}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\in\Sigma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{0}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash S{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{n}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\vdash T{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{0}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{(\text{max}\ n\ (m+1))}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash S{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{n}\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\vdash T{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{(m+1)}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash E\,E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\{x\mathrel{:=}E^{\prime}\}T\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}T\Gamma\vdash E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\Gamma\vdash S\equiv T\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{0}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\end{array}

Figure 7:
Typing for Computations

Type checking depends on these mutually dependent typing judgments:

•

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}
:
E
is of type
T
in context
\Gamma
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}}
:
\Psi
is a valid specification context in ctx.
\Gamma
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha\ {\texttt{is kind}}}
:
\alpha
is a syntactic type in ctx.
\Gamma
and spec. ctx.
\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K}
:
\alpha
is a syntactic type in ctx.
\Gamma
and spec. ctx.
\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha}
:
M
is of type
\alpha
in ctx.
\Gamma
and spec. ctx.
\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi}
:
\sigma
transports types from spec. context
\Phi
to ctx.
\Psi
.

and these definitional equality judgments:

•

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash E\equiv E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}
:
E
is equal to
E^{\prime}

at type
T
in context
\Gamma
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M\equiv N{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha}
:
M
is
equal to
N
at type
\alpha
in contexts
\Gamma
and
\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash K\equiv K^{\prime}}
:
K
is equal to
K^{\prime}
in
contexts
\Gamma
and
\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha\equiv\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K}
:
\alpha

is equal to
\beta
at kind
K
in contexts
\Gamma
and

\Psi
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\Psi\equiv\Phi{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}}
:
\Psi
is
equal to
\Phi
in context
\Gamma
.

•

\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\hat{\Psi}\equiv\hat{\Phi}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}}
:
Erased context
\hat{\Psi}
is equal to
\hat{\Phi}
in context
\Gamma
.

The typing for computations appears in Figure
7
and
the typing for specifications appears in Figures
8

and
9
. While rules for computational equality
appear in Figure
10
and for specifications in
Figures
11
,
12
, and

13
. Rules for equivalences and congruence rules are
omitted as they are as expected.

Most rules are as expected, however the interesting ones appear with a
star in their names. These rules, reproduced below deal with the
embedding of specifications in computations and vice versa.

\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{\sigma}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{[\sigma]}\,{\alpha}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Phi}\vdash{\alpha}]\end{array}

The rule
t-box
embeds an object together with its context in a
computation (of contextual type). And the
s-unbox
rule allows
specifications to refer to computations, in particular this is
essential to represent incomplete terms where the role of
meta-variables is taken by computational variables inside an unbox
construction.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\Psi$ is a valid specification context in ctx. $\Gamma$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\emptyset\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\lfloor{x}\rfloor\ {\texttt{is ctx}}x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\in\Gamma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\hat{\Psi}\ {\texttt{is erased}}}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\hat{\Psi}$ is a valid erased context in ctx. $\Gamma$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\emptyset\ {\texttt{is erased}}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\Psi,\,\widehat{x}\ {\texttt{is erased}}\Gamma\vdash\Psi\ {\texttt{is erased}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\lfloor{x}\rfloor\ {\texttt{is erased}}x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\in\Gamma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash K\ {\texttt{is kind}}}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\alpha$ is a syntactic kind in ctx. $\Gamma$ and spec. ctx. $\Psi$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\star\ {\texttt{is kind}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}K\ {\texttt{is kind}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\Gamma;\Psi,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash K\ {\texttt{is kind}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\alpha$ is a syntactic type in ctx. $\Gamma$ and spec. ctx. $\Psi$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\textbf{a}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K\textbf{a}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K\in\Sigma\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\Gamma;\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash{\alpha}\,{M}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[x\mathrel{:=}M]K\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}K\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K^{\prime}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash K\equiv K^{\prime}\end{array}

Typing Rules for Specifications (II)

2
Equality

Due to the presence of fully dependent types Orca’s computational
language does not have a distinction between type checking and
evaluation (as evaluation may happen at type check time). As usual,
the conversion rule (rule
t-conv
in Figure
7
) is
where there may be some computation to compare two types, that may
not be yet in normal form. Equality (and computation), as typing, is
shown in several judgments, one for the computational/reasoning
language and one for each syntactic category of the specification
language.

Again equality is defined in Figure
10
for
computations and for specifications in Figures
11
,

12
, and
13
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash E\equiv E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$E$ is equal to $E^{\prime}$ at type $T$ in context $\Gamma$}.\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash(\lambda x.E)\,E^{\prime}\equiv{\{x\mathrel{:=}{E^{\prime}}\}}\,{E}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\{x\mathrel{:=}{E^{\prime}}\}}\,{T}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma,x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\Gamma\vdash E^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{\lfloor{E}\rfloor_{{\texttt{id}}}}]\equiv E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\Gamma\vdash E{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M}]\equiv[\hat{\Phi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{M^{\prime}}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\hat{\Psi}\equiv\hat{\Phi}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash M\equiv M^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\equiv[{\Phi}\vdash{\beta}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{set}}_{0}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\equiv\Phi{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha\equiv\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\par\par\par\par\end{array}

Equality Rules for Computations

\Gamma;\Psi\vdash(\widehat{\lambda}x.M)\mathrel{\text{\textquotesingle}}N\equiv(\{\hat{x}\mathrel{:=}N\}M){\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\{\hat{x}\mathrel{:=}N\}\beta)\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash M{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta\Gamma;\Psi\vdash N{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\\[10.00002pt]

\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\mathbf{a}\equiv\mathbf{a}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}K\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta\equiv(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha^{\prime})\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha\equiv\alpha^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\Gamma;\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash\beta\equiv\beta^{\prime}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\par\\[10.00002pt]

Equality Rules for Specification Types and Kinds

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\Psi\equiv\Phi{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\Psi$ is equal to $\Phi$ in context $\Gamma$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\emptyset\equiv\emptyset{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\lfloor{x}\rfloor\equiv\lfloor{x}\rfloor{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\equiv\Phi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash\Psi\equiv\Phi{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\alpha\equiv\beta{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\star\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\hat{\Psi}\equiv\hat{\Phi}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}}\mathrel{:}\mbox{Erased context $\hat{\Psi}$ is equal to $\hat{\Phi}$ in context $\Gamma$.}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\emptyset\equiv\emptyset{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\lfloor{x}\rfloor\equiv\lfloor{x}\rfloor{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash\Psi,\,\widehat{x}\equiv\Phi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\Gamma\vdash\Psi\equiv\Phi{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\texttt{ctx}}\end{array}

Figure 13:
Equality Rules for Contexts

4
Definitions and Pattern Matching

In Section
3
, we extended a type theory with
contextual types and their introduction forms (i.e.: contextual
terms). However, to make use of these definitions it is necessary to
have an elimination form. To that effect in these section we extend
the language with top-level definitions by pattern matching. These
definitions take apart values by using simultaneous pattern matching
in the style of Agda
(
Norell 2007
)
.

\begin{array}[]{rlcll}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Patterns for Definitions}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Pattern}&P&\mathrel{::=}&{\mathbf{c}}\,{\vec{P}}&\text{A fully applied constant}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&x&\text{A variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&.P&\text{An inaccessible pattern}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{\mathcal{P}}]&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0} A contextual pattern}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Spec. Pattern}&\mathcal{P}&\mathrel{::=}&\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\,\,\vec{\mathcal{P}}&\text{A fully applied constant}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\widehat{\lambda}x.{\mathcal{P}}&\text{An abstraction}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\widehat{x}&\text{A bound variable}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&.\mathcal{P}&\text{An inaccessible pattern}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\lfloor{x}\rfloor_{\bar{\sigma}}&\text{\color[rgb]{1,0,0} An unboxed meta-variable}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Pattern Subst.}&\bar{\sigma}&\mathrel{::=}&{}^{\wedge}&\text{An empty substitution}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&{\texttt{id}}_{n}&\text{An identity substitution,}\\
&&&&\text{with weakening}\\
\end{array}

The syntax for patterns is straightforward, we highlight in red where
computations embed specifications and vice-versa. Notice, how in
patterns only variables can be unboxed as it is not clear what it
would mean to pattern match against a computation and not a value.
Furthermore, the substitution is weaker as only empty and identity
with weakening substitutions are allowed. Because we use fully applied
constructors for patterns we define the patterns
{\mathbf{c}}\,{\vec{P}}
and

\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\,\,\vec{\mathcal{P}}
that are applied to a spine of parameters.
The type-checking of pattern spines uses its own typing judgment where
we check a spine against a type that produces as a result the final
type of the constant applied to the pattern. Figure
14

shows the typing of patterns.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash P{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$P$ is of type $T$ in context $\Gamma$}.\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{\mathbf{c}}\,{\vec{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\lx@proof@logical@and\mathbf{c}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\in\Sigma\Gamma\vdash\vec{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\mathrel{\rangle}T\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}Tx{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\in\Gamma\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash.P{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\Gamma\vdash P{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash[\hat{\Psi}\mathrel{\triangleright}{\mathcal{P}}]{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[{\Psi}\vdash{\alpha}]\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma\vdash\overrightarrow{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\mathrel{\rangle}T}\mathrel{:}\mbox{Spine $\overrightarrow{P}$ is of type $S$ and produces type $T$ in context $\Gamma$}.\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma\vdash{P}\,{\vec{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)\mathrel{\to}S^{\prime}\mathrel{\rangle}T\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma\vdash P{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S\Gamma\vdash{\vec{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\{x\mathrel{:=}P\}}\,{S}^{\prime}\mathrel{\rangle}T\par\quad\par\Gamma\vdash\cdot{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\mathrel{\rangle}T\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha}\mathrel{:}\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$ is of type $\alpha$ in contexts $\Gamma$ and $\Psi$}.\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\widehat{\mathbf{c}}\,\,\vec{\mathcal{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{[\sigma]}\,{\beta}\lx@proof@logical@and\widehat{\mathbf{c}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\overrightarrow{(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)}\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta\in\Sigma\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\vec{\mathcal{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\vec{\alpha}\sigma=\overrightarrow{x:=\,\mathcal{P}_{i}}\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\widehat{\lambda}x.{\mathcal{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\beta\Gamma;\Psi,\,\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\vdash\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\in\Psi\par\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash.\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\lfloor{x}\rfloor_{\sigma}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{[\sigma]}\,{\alpha}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Phi\vdash x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\sigma{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\Phi\par\\[10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\boxed{\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\overrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\mathrel{\rangle}\beta}\mathrel{:}\mbox{Spine $\overrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}$ is of type $\alpha$ and produces type $\beta$ in $\Gamma$ and $\Psi$}.\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\Gamma;\Psi\vdash{\mathcal{P}}\,{\vec{\mathcal{P}}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}(\widehat{x}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha)\mathrel{\twoheadrightarrow}\alpha^{\prime}\mathrel{\rangle}\beta\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\mathcal{P}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\alpha\Gamma\vdash{\vec{\mathcal{P}}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}[x\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{P}]{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathrel{\rangle}\beta\par\quad\par\Gamma;\Psi\vdash\cdot{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\beta\mathrel{\rangle}\beta\end{array}

Figure 14:
Typing Rules for Patterns

Having defined patterns we move on to define top-level pattern
matching definitions:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Type annotation}&\mathbf{f}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\\
\text{Equations}&\Gamma_{1}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}_{1}=E_{1}\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\vdots\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\Gamma_{n}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}_{n}=E_{n}\\
\end{array}

A definition by pattern matching introduces a new constant and its
type followed by its pattern matching equations. Each equation
contains a context that binds all the variables used in the pattern, a
set of pattern and the right hand side of the equation that contains
the term that performs the computation when this equation is matched.
For each equation, patterns use every variable from the context
exactly once. Because non-linear patterns are unavoidable in dependent
pattern matching, all non-linear occurrences of a variable must be in
inside inaccessible patterns (where the value of the pattern is forced
by the type and thus is not really a discrimination pattern). This is
not enforced in the typing rules and has to be checked by the
implementation. Inaccessible patterns provide a nice way of
implementing the computation of pattern matching by using
discrimination trees, this is mostly a practical concern at this
point. The constant defined in a function by pattern matching may be
used inside the right hand side of any equations. As functions may
appear in types and be executed during type-checking, they should be
total. In this chapter we do not discuss how to check termination and
coverage to establish totality, but rather leave that as future work.
However the user is required to provide functions where pattern
matching is covering and that recursive calls are
terminating.

A valid pattern matching definition extends the signature and the
reduction behaviour of the system where each of the clauses becomes a
new reduction rule.

The typing of equations is done using the typing judgment in
Figure
15
that depends on the typing of patterns
presented in Figure
14
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\lx@intercol\boxed{\vdash\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P_{i}}=E_{i}\ {\texttt{valid}}}\hfil\lx@intercol:\text{The equation is valid}\\[10.00002pt]
\vdash\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}=E_{1}\ {\texttt{valid}}\lx@proof@logical@and\Gamma_{i}\vdash\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}_{1}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T\Gamma_{i}\vdash E_{i}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}T^{\prime}\Gamma_{i}\vdash T\equiv T^{\prime}\end{array}

Figure 15:
Typing Equations

Definitions by pattern matching extend the signature
\Sigma
with a
new constant (i.e.: the name of the function) and possibly multiple
pattern matching equations that become new computation rules. We
extend the syntax of the system as follows to accommodate for
equations:

\begin{array}[]{rlcll}\lx@intercol\hfil\boxed{\text{Extended Signature and Simultaneous Substitutions}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Signature}&\Sigma&\mathrel{::=}&\dots\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\Sigma,\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}_{i}=E_{i}&\text{Pattern matching equation}\\[10.00002pt]
\text{Substitution}&\theta&\mathrel{::=}&\cdot&\text{An empty substitution}\\
&&\mathrel{\mid}&\theta;x\mathrel{:=}E&\text{A substitution extension}\end{array}

Definitions by pattern matching compute by extending computation using
the equations in the definition in the following form:

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{f}\,\vec{E}\equiv{\{\theta\}}\,{E_{i}}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\{\theta\}}\,{T}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathbf{f}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}{\overrightarrow{(x{\,\mathrel{:}\,}S)}}\mathrel{\to}T\in\Sigma\Gamma\vdash\vec{E}{\,\mathrel{:}\,}\vec{S}\Gamma_{1}\mathrel{.}\mathbf{f}\,\vec{P}_{i}=E_{i}\in\Sigma\Gamma\vdash\vec{P}_{i}\doteq\vec{E}/\theta

This requires the computation of higher-order matching (i.e.:
operation
\Gamma\vdash P\doteq E/\theta
) between patterns and the
terms passed as parameter to the function. A sensible implementation,
(and it is indeed the Orca implementation does), tries the equations
in the order they where defined. This is done so as to respect the
user’s specified order in the case of overlapping patterns.

5
The Prototype Implementation

As seen in the example from Section
2
, the ideas from
Section
3
have been implemented in a prototype
language called Orca (following the marine mammal tradition of
languages like Beluga and Delphin
(
Poswolsky and Schürmann 2009
)
).
The OCaml implementation is available on

Github
3
3

3

https://github.com/orca-lang/orca
.

Parser

Well-formed Check

Type Reconstruction

Final AST

Implicit Parameters

Small Type Checker

Implemented

Planned

Figure 16:
The Orca Pipeline

Figure
16
shows the pipeline for the prototype. The
first line shows the existing pipeline, and the phases in the second
line show planned features that have not been implemented yet. The
first two phases form the front end of the language. First, the parser
produces the first version of the AST (Abstract Syntax Tree).
Subsequently, the AST generated by the parser is checked for some well
formed properties:

•

It disambiguates between constructors, variables, and bound variables.

•

It checks the scope of variables and definitions.

•

It transforms the bound variables of the specification framework
to de Bruijn indices.

The idea behind this design is to have a very permissive

parser
, and a second pass that removes ambiguities and can
report accurate error messages
4
4

4

Even if the current
implementation does not exploit this to have great error messages
.
The language does not enforce restrictions on naming conventions for
user defined identifiers. Constructors, variable names and definitions
can be composed of capitals, symbols and characters from any writing
system supported by the Unicode standard (with some restriction for
keywords and the symbols used in the grammar itself). The

well-formed check
phase checks that variables and definitions
respect their scopes and prepares an intermediate AST that is
susceptible to type reconstruction and checking. As a side note, this
phase is the perfect place to add mix-fix
parsing
(
Danielsson and Norell 2011
)
in the style of the Agda language.
As a remark, the lack of enforced conventions for names is done for
flexibility, but for large developments it would be important to
develop such a convention. We expect an ad-hoc convention to emerge
once larger Orca examples are implemented.

The third pass, type reconstruction, generates the final version of
the AST with all the type checking information included. This pass
performs two main tasks:

•

It disambiguates the computation and
specification terms by inserting boxes and un-boxes appropriately.

•

It simultaneously type checks user input.
